#4950. Crypto Market Structure Basics

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Exchanges, Liquidity, Orders, Derivatives, and Fundraising

Crypto market structure describes the venues, tools, participants, and settlement systems that allow digital assets to be bought, sold, priced, and transferred. It includes centralized and decentralized exchanges, order books, AMMs, trading pairs, market makers, derivatives, leverage, custody, and fundraising mechanisms.

Crypto markets offer a range of mechanisms and methods to trade crypto. Customers may choose to trade on centralized or decentralized platforms, and there are pros and cons associated with each. Here we look at the key components of the crypto market: the venues, actors, and financial products involved in driving price action.

We will consider the main features of crypto markets and what drives them, along with the various risks that traders should know about. Please note this piece is purely informative and should not be relied upon as a substitute for professional financial, legal or tax advice.

Below is a summary of the lessons in the course. For a detailed walk-through of each topic, please complete the interactive course.

Provides context around the range of venues, execution mechanisms, actors, products and associated risks in the crypto market.

Looks at the differences between CEXs and DEXs, and discusses order books, AMMs and liquidity pools.

Lesson 2 - Crypto Trading Pairs and Settlement

Describes the base and quote currencies within a pair, and the difference between on- and off-chain trade settlement.

Introduces the primary order types used to trade crypto (i.e. market, limit, stop, etc.) and some additional terms to be familiar with.

Lesson 4 - Longs, Shorts and Borrowed Exposure

Discusses the notion of going long or short crypto, using margin and trading on perpetual futures products.

Lesson 5 - Risks Associated with Derivatives

Considers the implications of highly leveraged positions and the impact of futures and options on the wider market.

Lesson 6 - Further Market Actors and Risks

Explores some of the other key market participants and additional forms of market abuse.

Presents an overview of the role played by VCs, PEs and alternative fundraising techniques in the crypto sector.

As mentioned above, crypto can be traded via either Centralized or Decentralized Exchanges.

As their name suggests, CEXs provide a service akin to a standard trading account. Customers will typically hold crypto with the CEX, which provides a venue to facilitate trades. In doing so, CEXs bridge the gap between one or more blockchains and their end-user base.

Alternatively, with DEXs, customers generally do not need to transfer their crypto into a centrally managed exchange account. Trading can take place directly from the customer's own wallet, most commonly facilitated via smart contracts.

Once we step away from the distinction between CEX and DEX, there are several mechanisms to bring together demand and supply for a given asset. Most obviously, many crypto exchanges will operate an order book. Users submit orders specifying the price at which they wish to purchase or sell the relevant asset. When compatible buy and sell orders are available, the exchange can match them and execute a trade.

A second approach is seen on many DEXs, where an AMM is employed. Liquidity pools comprise crypto assets deposited into smart contracts. When a user wishes to transact against these, they interact with the pool, at a price dictated by a mathematical function and the available liquidity.

Similar to FX, crypto is quoted in terms of a pair. For example, if someone buys the BTC/USDT pair, they will receive BTC and pay in USDT. BTC is the base currency and USDT is the quote currency, meaning the pair's price expresses how much USDT is required to purchase one BTC. Once a customer understands the composition of a trading pair, they will know what currency they are acquiring, giving up and at what price.

Finally, we must distinguish how a trade will be settled. CEXs typically maintain internal ledgers of their customers' holdings and execute trades without recording each transaction on the blockchain. On-chain transfers generally occur when assets enter or leave the exchange, while trades within the platform can be processed internally. On many DEXs, trades are executed and settled through smart contracts on the relevant blockchain, although parts of the order-matching or routing process may happen off-chain. The former approach will therefore tend to offer greater speed, whereas on-chain settlement is subject to the throughput and cost of the underlying blockchain.

Three common order types form the basis of the language used by crypto traders. A 'market' order instructs the platform to buy or sell immediately at the best available prices, although the final execution price will depend on available liquidity. With a 'limit' order, the trader specifies the maximum price they are willing to pay or the minimum price they will accept. Finally, a 'stop' order triggers another order once a certain price threshold is reached, although this does not guarantee execution at the trigger price.

There are further terms that traders will come across to denote their typical style of trading, such as 'scalping', 'swing' or 'buy and hold'. Again, these are simply descriptive and should not be construed as prescriptive.

A natural extension of the discussion above is that customers can take long or short positions on a crypto asset. Going long means taking a position that benefits if the asset appreciates in value, often by purchasing it directly. Conversely, a short position enables a trader to profit from a decline in the asset's price, although losses can arise if the price moves in the opposite direction.

By using borrowed funds or margin, a customer can increase their exposure beyond the capital they initially commit. While this can lead to greater returns, it also magnifies potential losses. Perpetual futures ('perps') are another product designed to allow traders to take exposure to the future price movements of an asset without a fixed expiry date. These use periodic funding payments between long and short positions to help keep contract prices aligned with the underlying market. Depending on the funding rate and their position, traders may either pay or receive funding.

If a large proportion of the market is exposed to high levels of leverage, the likelihood of forced liquidations can increase during sharp price movements. In turn, the buying or selling triggered by these events may place further upward or downward pressure on the market, potentially creating a liquidation cascade. Futures and options can provide a degree of protection when used to hedge existing exposures (and can of course be used to increase speculative exposure). However, they too carry considerable risk.

It will be apparent from the preceding discussion that customers require access to sufficient liquidity in order to efficiently enter and exit positions. On CEXs, market makers play an important role by submitting buy and sell orders, helping to maintain liquidity and reduce spreads. Similarly, many DEXs depend upon third parties supplying crypto to their liquidity pools, in return for a share of the trading fees generated.

There are a number of further considerations relating to the operation of crypto markets. Some of the more prominent forms of market manipulation are:

And finally, there are a number of other actors and entities that play a part in the crypto market: crypto custodians, information aggregators, government and supervisory authorities, and ETF issuers.

Finally, we touch upon some of the ways in which crypto businesses can secure finance. As with any industry, venture capital (VC) and private equity (PE) firms can invest in crypto-related ventures and provide a range of services beyond pure capital injection. Furthermore, there has been significant innovation in how crypto-native businesses can access funding. The sale of a project's native token(s) can occur via an ICO, allowing it to raise capital through token distribution. The advent of DEXs and CEXs has also enabled IDOs and IEOs respectively. In some cases, groups of investors can coalesce to form a DAO to collectively raise and deploy funds.

There are a number of key elements to understand in order to navigate the crypto market, spanning the range of venues, execution and settlement mechanisms, market actors and financial products.

Both CEXs and DEXs are capable of supporting customers, but differ materially in respect of custody and transparency.

Order books match compatible buy and sell orders, while AMMs use liquidity pools and algorithms to determine trading prices.

The decision to settle trades on- vs. off-chain will have material implications for customers.

Customers can use borrowing or derivatives to build larger positions, and the prevalence of leveraged exposure can create feedback loops throughout the market.

A diverse set of organizations underpin the crypto market, and will continue to evolve over time.

Lastly, the range of fundraising avenues open to crypto businesses also warrants careful consideration.

Market structure: The various mechanisms, market actors, products, rules and economic incentives governing the trading and pricing of an asset.

Centralized Exchange (CEX): A platform that facilitates trading and will typically hold customer assets or balances in custodial accounts.

Decentralized Exchange (DEX): A platform or protocol that enables customers to conduct trades directly from their wallets, commonly through smart contracts.

Order Book: Records the current set of outstanding bids and offers, allowing compatible buy and sell orders to be matched.

Bid: The price a buyer is willing to pay for an asset.

Offer: The price a seller is willing to accept for an asset.

Automated Market Maker (AMM): Allows trading against a pool of assets, with prices determined by a predefined algorithm.

Liquidity: The capacity to transact in an asset without materially affecting its price.

Slippage: The extent to which a trade's execution price differs from the anticipated price.

Settlement: The process of completing a trade and transferring or recording the resulting asset ownership or balances.

Leverage: The use of borrowed funds or derivatives to increase a customer's market exposure relative to their committed capital.

Perpetual Future: A futures-style instrument that does not have a fixed maturity. Generally uses periodic funding payments to help align its price with the underlying market.

Funding Rate: The rate used to calculate periodic payments between long and short positions in perpetual futures markets. Depending on the rate, either side may pay or receive funding.

Liquidation: The forced closing or reduction of a leveraged position when its available margin or collateral falls below the required threshold.

Market Maker: A firm or entity that provides buy and sell quotes to a market, helping maintain liquidity.

Initial Coin Offering (ICO): A fundraising mechanism through which a project offers tokens to participants in exchange for capital.

Decentralized Autonomous Organization (DAO): A structure allowing members to take collective decisions based on governance rules, which may include token-weighted voting.

Crypto market structure is the set of venues, participants, tools, settlement systems, and incentives that determine how digital assets are traded and priced.

A CEX is an intermediary platform that usually holds user balances, while a DEX lets users trade from their own wallets through smart contracts.

An order book lists bids from buyers and asks from sellers. When prices align, a trade can execute.

An AMM is a smart-contract mechanism that lets traders swap against a liquidity pool instead of a traditional order book.

Leverage increases the size of a position using borrowed funds or derivative exposure, which can amplify both gains and losses.

A perpetual future is a derivative contract without a fixed expiration date, usually kept near the spot market through funding payments.

They are crypto-native fundraising models that use token sales or exchange infrastructure to raise capital and distribute tokens.

#4949. Bitcoin Lender Ledn Moves US Clients Onshore With New Custody Accounts

Bitcoin lender Ledn is moving beyond loans. The company launched Ledn Custody on Tuesday, a new account that lets eligible U.S. clients hold bitcoin, gold, stablecoins and U.S. dollars, and buy, sell and swap between them in one place, with no trading fees and a 0.5% spread.

Ledn says that spread makes buying bitcoin through its app about half the cost of a standard purchase on the most popular U.S. bitcoin apps. The launch also moves Ledn’s U.S. clients, previously served from its Cayman Islands entity, to a U.S. company with a U.S. custodian.

Ledn has lent against bitcoin since 2018 and has serviced more than $11 billion in loans. Until now, most of its U.S. clients bought their bitcoin elsewhere and moved it to Ledn to borrow against it. The new account brings that first purchase in-house, from buying through to borrowing.

A standard bitcoin purchase on the most popular U.S. apps costs about 0.9% to 1.25% once trading fees and spread are included, according to Ledn’s own analysis of published fees from October. That puts Ledn in direct competition with the trading apps where many Americans buy their first bitcoin.

On a $1,000 purchase, that works out to about $5 at Ledn against $9 to $12.50 elsewhere. Clients can deposit dollars and hold them in the account, or use them to buy bitcoin, gold and stablecoins. BitGo describes the gold option as tokenized gold.

“What our clients want is to hold hard assets, whether that’s bitcoin, gold or stablecoins,” Ledn co-founder and Chief Strategy Officer Mauricio Di Bartolomeo said. “Most people buy them in one place, keep them in another, and when they need cash, they sell. Now they can do all three in one account.”

He added that clients can borrow against their bitcoin instead of selling, “with gold to follow.”

Assets in a Ledn Custody account are held for the benefit of each client by an independent qualified custodian, segregated from Ledn’s own assets and never lent out for interest. BitGo Bank & Trust, a unit of BitGo (BTGO), provides the custody and trading services behind the account.

The custody setup is a selling point for a lender that came through 2022. Ledn says it has returned every client’s collateral across two full market cycles, including the collapse that bankrupted Celsius, BlockFi and Voyager. The company has also published monthly loan book and proof of reserves data.

“We’ve spent eight years earning our clients’ trust as a lender,” Ledn co-founder and CEO Adam Reeds said. “If you’re going to be a financial services company, boring is a good thing. We want to be the reliable, stable place where people build their wealth, not a platform with a million features built for trading.”

The onshore move lines up with Washington’s message. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said in May that crypto’s problems stem from “the wild, wild west offshore,” adding, “we got to bring it onshore.” The Clarity Act, the market structure bill he urged Congress to pass, stalled in the Senate in September.

Ledn is chasing a big market. More than 67 million Americans now own crypto, or about one in four U.S. adults, according to a May survey. Competition for bitcoin borrowers is also heating up, with Cross River Bank launching its first bitcoin-backed loans this week.

Ledn has leaned on the capital markets to fund its lending, selling $188 million in bitcoin-backed bonds in February. The custody account now gives the lender a way to win clients before they ever need a loan.

Ledn Custody accounts will roll out to eligible U.S. individual clients over the coming days. The company plans to add lending against gold and open more U.S. states in the coming weeks.

#4947. Ethereum treasury firm BTCS sells $43.8 million in ETH to cut Aave debt, CEO says ‘we do not take this lightly’

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The repayment leaves the company holding about 24,245 ETH, alongside $17.9 million in convertible notes due in 2027.

BTCS sold about 27,300 ETH for $43.8 million on Nov. 20 and used the proceeds to repay the same amount of borrowing on Aave, the company said in a Form 8-K filed with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday.

The sale implies an average price of roughly $1,604 per ETH. It came as the world's second-largest crypto asset by market cap slid about 12% from its intraday high to $1,561 on Nov. 20, when Bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) fell below $60,000 for the first time since July, and the Nasdaq Composite dropped 5.3%.

"Friday was a hard day for the markets and for BTCS," Charles Allen said in a statement. "As ETH fell, we chose to sell ETH and pay down our Aave borrowings rather than carry that debt through a falling market. Selling at these prices came at a real cost to the Company and its shareholders, and we do not take that lightly."

The company sold to limit the risk of liquidation and meet obligations under its senior secured convertible notes, Allen said, adding that the measures would put BTCS on a more durable financial footing.

After the latest sale, BTCS estimated it held about 24,245 ETH and cash and digital assets worth about $61 million, based on an ETH price of $1,722 on Nov. 22.

The company also had about $17.9 million of senior secured convertible notes outstanding, with maturities in May and July 2027.

BTCS temporarily pulled its Imperium DeFi business out of liquidity pools during the volatility. The company said the capital earns no liquidity-pool fees while undeployed and that it intends to redeploy it when conditions improve.

Imperium generated $3.5 million of revenue in the third quarter, accounting for about 86% of BTCS’s $4.1 million in total revenue.

BTCS shares fell 6.26% on Monday, extending a 77.41% year-to-date decline. Ethereum has declined 28.16% over the past month and 42.99% year to date.

#4945. Tom Lee Says Ethereum Is as Scarce as Bitcoin—And Bitmine Just Bought More

Tom Lee Says Ethereum Is as Scarce as Bitcoin—And Bitmine Just Bought More

Bitmine Immersion Technologies kept its weekly Ethereum buying streak alive, adding 7,819 ETH worth roughly $13.8 million, as chairman Tom Lee leaned on a new argument for the battered token: its supply grows no faster than Bitcoin’s, making it just as much “hard money.”

The NYSE-listed company said Monday its holdings reached 6,085,564 ETH, worth roughly $10.4 billion at a reference price of $1,713 per coin, as of Nov. 22.

That’s 4.98% of Ethereum’s 122.2 million token supply, leaving Bitmine 99% of the way to its goal of owning 5% of the network, the “Alchemy of 5%” target it has chased for 17 months. The firm has bought Ethereum every week since launching its treasury strategy on June 30, 2025.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Ethereum with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Lee’s pitch this week centered on scarcity. Since Bitmine pivoted to Ethereum in mid-2025, he said, ETH’s supply has grown just 0.90% on an annualized basis, roughly in line with Bitcoin’s 0.82%, meaning “both are ‘hard money’ measured in supply growth.” (Disclosure: Tom Lee is one of several investors in Dastan, Decrypt’s parent company.)

He contrasted that with more inflationary rivals, citing annualized supply growth of 7.6% for Solana, 5.4% for XRP, and 2.9% for Cardano. The framing builds on Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin’s recent declaration that ETH is a store of value, which Lee has repeatedly invoked.

Counting cash, other tokens and “moonshot” investments, Bitmine’s total holdings stood at $11.1 billion, including 216 Bitcoin, $384 million in cash and marketable securities, a $180 million stake in Beast Industries and a $54 million position in Eightco Holdings.

Bitmine remains the world’s largest Ethereum treasury and the second-largest crypto treasury overall, behind Michael Saylor’s Strategy, which the release said holds 849,327 BTC.

Strategy also kept buying through the downturn, recently adding $30 million in Bitcoin while shifting $1 billion from its dollar reserve to a cash pot.

The purchase lands as crypto claws back some ground after a bruising November. Bitcoin traded around $62,700 on Monday, up about 3.2% on the day, with Ethereum near $1,769, up 3.5%, as risk appetite steadied.

The rebound coincided with a dramatic reversal at Nvidia, which reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO just days after its board had ruled out his return, lifting sentiment across the AI-linked assets that crypto increasingly tracks.

Bitmine has 5,227,037 ETH, about 86% of its holdings, staked through its MAVAN platform, with projected annualized staking revenue of roughly $245 million.

#4944. Bitcoin ETFs keep bleeding as BlackRock’s IBIT shares gain nearly 5%

BlackRock’s IBIT shares gained 4.85% on Monday despite continued Bitcoin ETF withdrawals that pushed November’s net outflows to $4.71 billion.

US-listed spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) extended their outflow streak to five consecutive trading days amid a rebound in shares of the largest Bitcoin fund, BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT).

The funds recorded $569.7 million in net outflows on Monday, bringing November’s total so far to $4.71 billion, according to SoSoValue data. The withdrawals have surpassed June’s record of $4.51 billion, putting the funds on track for their worst month since launching in January 2024.

IBIT shares gained 4.85% on Monday to close at $34.62 before slipping 1.3% in overnight trading, Yahoo Finance data showed. Meanwhile, Bitcoin (BTC) fell 1.8% over 24 hours to around $60,450, erasing Monday’s rally following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s reinstatement.

US spot Bitcoin ETFs have recorded $2.37 billion in net outflows over the past five trading sessions, led by IBIT’s $1.95 billion in withdrawals. The BlackRock fund broke its daily outflow record twice last week.

Bitwise’s Camran Khosravi attributed IBIT’s record outflows to a broader retreat from risk assets amid tech and artificial intelligence sector concerns and hawkish Federal Reserve signals.

“The largest funds naturally see the largest flows in both directions, and even record daily outflows are small relative to their total assets,” Khosravi told Cointelegraph.

US spot Bitcoin ETFs held $73.6 billion in combined net assets as of Monday, with IBIT holding $45.64 billion, according to WalletPilot data.

#4942. Europol operation involving Binance, Coinbase and Chainalysis traces more than $28.5 million in crypto behind illegal streaming services

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Europol said police disrupted 57 IPTV services, and analysis of the cryptocurrency transactions is ongoing.

Binance, Coinbase, and Chainalysis joined police from 11 countries for a five-day operation aimed at the financial networks of illegal IPTV streaming services, with more than €26 million ($28.5 million) in cryptocurrency transactions traced so far, Europol said in a statement on Tuesday.

Investigators met at the Spanish National Police headquarters in Seville from Nov. 16 to Nov. 20. Europol said the operation disrupted 57 illegal IPTV services and identified 321 websites illegally offering copyrighted content, with combined annual traffic of more than 7.3 million visits.

For a few euros a month, illegal IPTV services sell access to thousands of channels, live sports, and films without paying the rights holders, according to the statement, often collecting subscription payments through cryptocurrency.

The Audio-visual Anti-Piracy Alliance estimates 60 million people across Europe use the unauthorized services, with legitimate providers losing €4.83 billion ($5.29 billion) each year and governments €1.08 billion ($1.18 billion) in unpaid taxes.

During the operation, investigators followed suspicious transactions across blockchain networks, looking for connections between illegal streaming services, cryptocurrency wallets, and the individuals controlling them, Europol said.

The operation involved around 40 participants, including 30 law enforcement officers from Belgium, Bulgaria, Croatia, Greece, Ireland, Italy, the Netherlands, Portugal, Romania, Spain and the United States. Interpol, the European Union Intellectual Property Office, and the Audio-visual Anti-Piracy Alliance also participated.

Europol said its analysis is continuing and information gathered will also support national investigations, including efforts to identify suspects, disrupt illegal services, and trace assets linked to criminal activity.

In January 2024, German authorities seized 50,000 BTC worth nearly $2.2 billion in a separate piracy case, the largest amount of bitcoin ever seized by German law enforcement at the time.

#4941. I Test Outdoor Gear for a Living—Here Are 6 Gifts Nature Lovers Will Actually Use

Tractor Supply Co. has plenty for the outdoorsy set—including these top picks.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

TESTING HIKING BOOTS, trail shoes, camping gear, and the like for a living means I spend a lot of time outdoors. Living in the Edwin B. Forsythe National Wildlife Refuge in coastal New Jersey, I’m fortunate to be able to step out my door and immediately be right in the middle of nature.

And when the weather starts to get colder, I take that as a cue not to head indoors, but to layer up. That means the hooded wetsuit goes on, an assortment of puffer jackets get put through the paces, and base layers become the first line of defense. With the holiday season upon us, here are six gifts that fellow nature lovers will find useful in the coming year. Better yet—they’re all available for purchase at Tractor Supply Co.

While Yeti’s coolers are certainly premium investments, the brand’s drinkware comprises some of its most accessible, hard-wearing products. Yes, the Yeti Rambler Water Bottle has been our best water bottle since it was released, but an insulated version is a must for anyone looking to stay outdoors. This 20-ounce stackable one is great because you can keep a few stacked in your camp kitchen gear and always know you’ve got mugs for the campsite.

The Carhartt knit beanie has experienced a resurgence in popularity over the past couple winters, and for good reason: It’s affordable, it’s comfy in an acrylic knit, it comes in a ton of great colors, and it’s gonna last for years. While you really can’t go wrong with any color, I’m pretty partial to the traditional Carhartt brown, which evokes the iconic canvas Duck Jacket.

My hands get pretty thrashed throughout the year surfing in the cold and constantly being outside, and the harsh, dry winter air is not very kind to them. I do my best with wearing gloves in the cold, but when I’m shooting photos of wildlife or practicing other hobbies that call for maximum dexterity, I sometimes fall short. That means I eventually get some cracked knuckles and need a repair balm like this aptly named option from Duke Cannon. It’s made with lanolin, which means it doesn’t leave my hands sticky or greasy like other lotions and balms tend to do.

Speaking of gloves, if I’m outside and not being super active (say, just walking the dog), or need to protect my hands while doing yardwork or bringing firewood into the house for the wood-burning stove, I turn to a leather pair like these. They tend to wear in, not out. And you can always just sport some cheapy knit gloves underneath if you want more insulation. Hearty gloves like this are always a must-have for campsite duty.

The nature preserve I live in is part of a pretty active salt-marsh tidal zone, and a creek runs behind my house that I launch my canoe and paddleboard from. This way of life requires me to be cognizant of (and really in tune with) the tides. It also requires a good pair of muck boots, because some days even just walking the dog means trekking through water. Deck boots (or those shorter ones that go just above the ankle) are pretty pointless in this sort of area—you really want something taller like these knee-highs, which are insulated and keep you dry all day long.

A thin (read: not puffy) vest is an overlooked layering option for the outdoors. It can function as an outer layer on warmer days, and when it cools off it still fits seamlessly under a shell or puffer. This reversible Field & Stream option is a solid choice because it’s water-resistant on one side and has soft polyester fleece on the other (adding even more insulation). And that cuffed neck is great for full coverage when it gets chilly.

Ryan Brower is the Senior Gear and Reviews Editor at Men’s Health, where he tests the best products in outdoor gear, tech, running shoes, and more. He has written about the outdoors for nearly two decades and was previously the Managing Editor, Content Performance at Gear Patrol. He holds a B.A. in Sport Management and is a Certified Beer Server, having previously covered the craft beer industry. Ryan currently lives on the water in New Jersey with his wife and their dog.

#4939. Jensen Huang’s Wealth Rises $36 Billion as Nvidia Surge Burns Short Sellers for $12 Billion

Jensen Huang’s Wealth Rises $36 Billion as Nvidia Surge Burns Short Sellers for $12 Billion

Jensen Huang’s net worth surged by more than $36 billion on Monday after Nvidia’s board reinstated him as CEO, a dramatic reversal that sent the chipmaker’s stock rocketing and handed its short sellers their worst day in years.

Nvidia shares jumped more than 33% in Monday morning trading, to around $167, recovering the ground lost after Huang resigned 11 days earlier amid a scandal over extramarital affairs, including one with a woman U.S. officials reportedly suspected of spying for China.

The rebound added roughly $36.6 billion to Huang’s fortune, according to Forbes, lifting the co-founder, who owns an estimated 3.5% of Nvidia, to 13th on its global wealth ranking.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Nvidia with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

On the other side of the trade, investors betting against Nvidia were nursing about $12.2 billion in paper losses, their biggest single-day hit since at least 2019, according to data firm ORTEX cited by Reuters.

The reversal wiped out more than $10.5 billion in gains those short sellers had booked just last week. Still, ORTEX noted the bears remain up roughly $14 billion since Oct. 29, and with only about 1% of Nvidia’s float sold short, co-founder Peter Hillerberg characterized the move as a reaction to the news rather than a full short squeeze.

The catalyst was Nvidia’s board voting unanimously to bring Huang back, less than two weeks after his abrupt exit. The return followed President Donald Trump publicly pushing for it, with the president saying the board had sought “assurance on the national security threat” tied to the espionage allegations.

The reinstatement rippled well beyond Nvidia. The news lifted the broader market and dragged crypto up with it, with Bitcoin rallying as risk appetite returned, even as Anthropic shares sank.

The S&P 500 climbed about 5% and the Nasdaq rose nearly 7% on Monday morning, clawing back losses from a Friday selloff driven by anxiety over AI spending.

The episode caps one of the strangest corporate sagas in recent memory: a founder toppled by a spy-tinged scandal, replaced, then restored within days as the president intervened and markets whipsawed on every turn.

For Huang, it marks a remarkable personal recovery, his wealth and his role both snapping back almost overnight.

For Nvidia, whose chips underpin both the AI boom and the data centers increasingly entwined with crypto, the saga is a reminder of how much the market’s fortunes hinge on a single company, and a single person.

#4934. Perps and Swaps: How to Trade the News on Decrypt

Perps and Swaps: How to Trade the News on Decrypt

Readers have traditionally learned about markets in one place and traded them in another. A story about Bitcoin, a public company or a commodity might shape a view, but acting on it meant opening an exchange or a trading app and starting over.

Decrypt set out to close that gap in September 2026, when it announced The Information Exchange: a network that brings together news, data, market prices, probabilities and the ability to trade, around the subjects readers follow.

The Information Exchange sees Decrypt delivering both the reporting and the market infrastructure that links information to trading, while the Solana blockchain provides the network on which trades settle, including token swaps and perpetual futures.

This guide explains what each one is and how to trade it on Decrypt.

Everything on The Information Exchange runs through a Decrypt Money Account, which launched on Solana in October 2026.

A Money Account holds one balance that funds every product on Decrypt: swaps, perpetual futures and prediction markets.The account is self-custodial, meaning Decrypt does not hold users' private keys and cannot act on their behalf. It is designed to work without seed phrases, and network fees are covered, so users don't need to hold SOL, Solana's native token, to pay for transactions.

A swap exchanges one token for another at the current market price. Instead of placing an order on an exchange and waiting for it to fill, the trader sends one token and receives another in a single transaction.

Swaps on Decrypt are powered by Jupiter, a Solana trading platform that offers swaps, lending, borrowing, perpetual futures and portfolio tools. Jupiter finds prices across Solana's trading venues, so users don't need to choose where a trade is executed.

At time of publication, swaps cover a select group of major cryptocurrencies and Solana tokens, as well as tokenized stocks, commodities and other real-world assets.

As of November 2026, the Assets panel of the Portfolio page groups holdings into crypto, stocks, commodities and collectibles, and lists tokens including SOL, BTC, ETH, XRP and HYPE.

Perpetual futures, or perps, are contracts that let traders bet on whether an asset's price will rise or fall without owning the asset. Unlike traditional futures, they have no expiry date. A trader who expects the price to rise goes "long," and one who expects it to fall goes "short."

Perps are usually traded with leverage, which lets a trader control a larger position than their deposit would otherwise allow. Leverage magnifies gains and losses alike, and if the market moves against a position far enough, it can be liquidated, or closed automatically, wiping out the trader's margin.

Perps on Decrypt are powered by Phoenix, a fully onchain perpetuals exchange on Solana developed by Ellipsis Labs—making Decrypt the first media platform to offer perps trading alongside its journalism.

As of November 2026, Phoenix offers more than 100 perps markets on Decrypt, covering crypto, U.S. stocks, gold and pre-IPO companies. Markets trade around the clock, with leverage of up to 40x, according to Phoenix.

Decrypt plans to keep adding products to The Information Exchange, including Situation Rooms: pages that combine breaking news, live data, prices, probabilities and trading around a single subject.

Decrypt operates The Information Exchange, Decrypt Money Accounts and the swaps, perpetual futures and prediction market products described in this guide.

Perpetual futures are leveraged products and carry a high risk of loss. Availability may vary by region. Nothing in this guide is investment advice.

#4929. Solo Bitcoin Miner Scores Again, Hitting $191,000 Jackpot Against the Odds

Solo Bitcoin Miner Scores Again, Hitting $191,000 Jackpot Against the Odds

A lone Bitcoin miner has once again defied steep odds, single-handedly mining a block and pocketing a reward worth roughly $191,000, a windfall that normally flows to the industrial-scale operations dominating the network.

Blockchain data shows the miner solved block 977057 on Nov. 22, earning 3.12871270 BTC, made up of the 3.125 BTC base subsidy plus about 0.0037 BTC in transaction fees.

The block was found through Solo CKPool, a service that lets independent miners point their machines at the network and keep the entire reward if they get lucky, rather than pooling hash power and sharing payouts.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Solo miners command a tiny sliver of Bitcoin’s total computing power, and finding a block means being the first to guess a winning number against the combined might of massive mining farms running warehouses full of specialized machines.

The odds for a small setup are often likened to winning a lottery, which is why each solo success tends to draw attention.

And yet, it’s far from an isolated event, and solo Bitcoin miners have hit jackpots like this with increasing regularity throughout 2026. Scores worth around $300,000, $225,000, $210,000 and $200,000, as the price of Bitcoin fluctuates, have all landed with individual miners over the past year, some using only modest or rented hardware.

The frequency underscores that while the probability for any single miner is minuscule, the sheer number of people trying means someone occasionally beats the house.

The timing is notable against the backdrop of a strained mining industry. Bitcoin miners have been selling their holdings and pivoting infrastructure toward the AI boom, squeezed by thin margins, a lower post-halving subsidy and a Bitcoin price that has slumped well off its highs.

For large operators, those pressures have made mining a tougher business; for the solo miner who just won, it’s a rare, outsized payday.

What a solo miner does with the reward is up to them, and the win doesn’t change the economics for everyone else still grinding away. But these occasional long-shot victories remain a point of fascination in Bitcoin, a reminder that the network’s lottery, however improbable, still pays out to the little guy now and then.

At current prices, with Bitcoin trading around $62,000, the 3.13 BTC reward works out to roughly $191,000, though the dollar value shifts with the market.

#4925. Robinhood co-leads $5 million seed round for onchain trading platform Rialto

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Tandem, the venture arm of Offchain, the company behind Arbitrum, co-led the round with Robinhood.

Rialto, an onchain trading platform for crypto and tokenized assets, has raised $5 million in a strategic seed round.

Robinhood and Tandem, the venture arm of Arbitrum developer Offchain, co-led the round, Rialto said Tuesday. They were the only investors, Rialto co-founder Can told The Block. Rialto began raising in October 2025 and closed the round in early 2026, Can said. The platform has announced the funding now as it emerged from stealth after a year of development, during which it said it worked closely with Tandem, Offchain and Robinhood.

It was Rialto's first funding round and was structured as equity with token warrants, Can said. He did not disclose the valuation or answer whether Rialto plans to launch a token or raise more funding in the near future.

Can said that is his real first name but declined to disclose his full name. Another co-founder, who goes by Riley, also declined to disclose their full name. Can said most of the Rialto team comes from a high-frequency trading background and has experience building low-latency trading systems.

"That's the experience we're bringing to onchain markets," Can said. "Onchain markets have demonstrated the benefits of transparency and composability, but the next phase requires trading infrastructure that can deliver competitive liquidity and execution at scale."

Rialto offers spot crypto trading and more than 200 Robinhood Stock Tokens on Robinhood Chain. The platform supports tokenized assets linked to equities, ETFs and commodities, and aims to offer deeper liquidity, competitive prices, low fees and better trade execution.

Can said Rialto relies on its proprietary market-making infrastructure and routing technology. "Our PropAMM-driven approach puts market-making and inventory management directly onchain, so professional market makers can quote competitive prices," he said.

Rialto's router looks across trading venues for the best available price, Can said, noting that a recent upgrade cut average quote response times from 250 milliseconds to 20 milliseconds. "Faster quotes track the market more closely, which means tighter prices for traders on the Rialto app and for platforms using the Swap API," he said.

"Bringing onchain assets together with competitive liquidity and fast execution is the kind of work that helps the Robinhood Chain ecosystem grow, and we're excited to back the team," Ben Einstein, director of corporate and business development at Robinhood, said in a statement.

Asked about competitors, Can said Rialto's Swap API competes with aggregators on liquidity and speed on Robinhood Chain, and its app is designed to compete with centralized platforms on performance and fees. Rialto earns revenue from a 5-basis-point trading fee on the app, Can said. He did not share revenue figures.

Rialto reported $199.3 million in trading volume in October, up from $53.4 million in September. Stock token trading volume rose 329% from September to $183.8 million in October, the platform said. Can said Rialto has processed more than $600 million in spot trading volume across its app and Swap API since launching publicly in July 2026.

Asked about expansion to other blockchains, Can said Robinhood Chain remains Rialto's priority, and its Swap API is also live on Arbitrum. He said Rialto is working with firms in both ecosystems to support more assets as they come onchain.

"Our focus is on working with Robinhood and Offchain to make Rialto the go-to place to trade on Robinhood Chain and a core aggregator for Arbitrum," Can said.

"We think the Robinhood Chain has a big opportunity to bring a new class of users onchain, and to give them execution that matches what they're used to on centralized platforms," Can added. "We have no other chains to announce."

Rialto said it will use the funding for product development, hiring, and expansion. Can said the company was founded in mid-2025 and has fewer than 10 people across Europe and Asia, most of them engineers. It is hiring for backend engineering, marketing, and business development.

#4923. South Korea proposes bank-led stablecoins, 20% cap on crypto exchange stakes

The draft bill would also reopen domestic initial coin offerings after a ban dating to 2017 and require issuers to disclose risks before soliciting buyers.

South Korea’s Financial Services Commission (FSC) unveiled a draft bill Tuesday that would initially limit issuance of won-denominated stablecoins to groups majority-owned by banks and generally cap shareholders’ stakes in crypto exchanges at 20%.

Banks would have to own more than half of a stablecoin issuer collectively, though a fintech or technology company could be its largest individual shareholder, the FSC said in its announcement.

Domestic stablecoin issuers would need FSC authorization and at least 5 billion won (about $3.7 million) in equity capital. They would also have to hold reserves covering at least 100% of their outstanding tokens and redeem them at face value without delay.

Exchanges would need dealing, brokerage and custody licenses and would have to meet capital and operating requirements. Shareholders’ stakes would be capped at 20%, or 34% with FSC approval.

The draft also seeks to lift South Korea’s 2017 ban on domestic initial coin offerings.

Regulators previously aimed to produce the bill in 2025, the year President Lee Jae-myung campaigned on introducing a won-denominated stablecoin.

Work stalled over who could issue the tokens. The central bank wanted banks to control issuers, while the FSC favored allowing nonbank companies to issue them as well. The proposed cap on exchange ownership also drew opposition from lawmakers.

Related: South Korea lawmakers mull pushing crypto tax implementation to 2029

#4920. The 12 Best Swim Trunks for Men of 2026, Worn and Tested by Style Editors

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Our editors recommend shorts from Rhone and Fair Harbor.

SUMMER MAY BE behind us, but that doesn’t mean your swim trunks need to go into hibernation. If you’re planning to escape the cold for somewhere sunny this winter, a good pair should be in your suitcase. The beauty of men’s swim trunks is that they can be worn in or out of the water, doubling as the ultimate beachside bottoms or everyday shorts. The best swim trunks for men today have a moisture-wicking and quick-drying fabric, four-way stretch for mobility, a flattering length, and an athletic fit. Finding the right one for you can be a challenge, which is why we're here to help. Our team of fashion editors spent the past year trying and testing swim trunks to find options that blend comfort and style.

Over the past four years, I’ve tested more than 20 different pairs of swim trunks—everything from legacy surf brands to buzzy newcomers—but Fair Harbor’s have consistently exceeded my expectations. This pair nails that trifecta of being stylish, functional, and worth the price.

The first thing I noticed: the fabric. It’s a cotton-poly blend that feels smooth and broken-in right out of the gate. It doesn’t feel like any of that stiff, swishy, overly synthetic feel you get with most swim trunks. I’m usually quick to write off built-in liners altogether, but the BreezeKnit liner changed my mind. It feels more like a supportive pair of boxer briefs than the usual scratchy mesh. It’s snug (but comfortable on the crown jewels), breathable, and, most importantly, it doesn’t ride up or chafe. I’ve worn it for full beach days in Puerto Rico—swimming, walking, grabbing lunch—and never once felt the need to adjust.

The 7-inch inseam hits just above the knee on me, giving it a clean, tailored look. It’s an easy middle ground that works whether you’re getting in the water or just wearing it as your default summer short. There’s enough stretch in the cotton-polyester fabric that I never feel restricted. Plus, it comes in seersucker versions, solid-colored options, and other unique patterns. The hidden zipper pocket stashes a key or card without them slipping out, and the side pockets don’t balloon up in the water—something a lot of trunks still get wrong. It’s not a substitute for a gym short, but the fabric dries quickly for other oceanside activities.

I can’t get enough of the R&R Trunk's timeless style, functional fabric, and flattering fit. I love the no-fuss design with a tailored cut that’s not too baggy or slim. Coupled with the flexible waistband, the fit looks great on most body types. It’s available in two inseams–5.5 and 7.5 inches. The latter provides enough coverage and hits a couple inches above the knee for most men (our universal style recommendation for shorts). Though I wore the shorter length on the beach during my stay in Miami because it shows off my summer leg gains.

Rhone’s athleisure performs just as well as it looks. I’ve sweat-tested the R&R during beachside runs and volleyball games, and found that its odor-fighting fabric kept me feeling fresh. The mesh liner kept my family jewels locked in, without cutting off circulation. A few editors on our style team who have the R&R trunk say that the liner really does have a second-skin like quality. That’s probably because the polyester blend fabric is stretchy, which felt more comfortable than Lululemon’s (also great!) Swim Short. These durable features also mean the R&R Trunk doubles as a decent gym short if you’re in a pinch, and saves you one less thing to pack for a tropical vacation. We love that it comes in 11 colorways, but if you want to go classic, you can’t go wrong with the Electric Blue or Navy option.

The absence of liner offers a comfortable commando style, which is why I prefer a pair like this one from Lululemon. I don’t have to be bothered with any mesh material that feels restrictive or scratchy against my crown jewels—I can just slip it on and head into the water. I also love that the minimal design doesn’t scream “swim trunk”, so I can go from brunch to the boardwalk or the pool without having to do an outfit change. It passes as an everyday short, which is exactly the kind of versatility I want in the summer. The polyester-elastomultiester fabric has so much stretch, it performs just as good—if not better—than most of my gym shorts.

I’ve worn this pair for pickup volleyball and quick workouts, and they move with you instead of clinging or sagging out. It also dries fast and doesn't get that heavy, waterlogged feel after a swim, which makes a huge difference if you’re posted up poolside for a while. The 7-inch inseam is short enough to show some quad, but long enough that it still feels put-together if you’re grabbing a drink after. On my 5’8” frame, it lands a couple inches above the knee with a clean, tailored look—not too baggy, not too slim. If you’re taller, it’ll read a bit shorter and sportier; shorter guys get that ideal mid-thigh cut without going full retro.

After testing Proof’s Performance Swim Trunk, we found that it’s easily the most durable short on this list. Our editors appreciate the polyester-spandex blend fabric, which is stretchy, quick drying, and nearly tear proof. We’ve sported this during tropical hikes and extreme water sports, and found that the Performance Trunk held up to just about anything mother nature threw at it. We like the moisture-wicking liner, which is actually more comfortable than Rhone’s R&R short, but we did find it didn’t dry as quickly as Rhone’s short, and is a bit thicker than we’d prefer. Still, those are minor quibbles for a swim trunk as functional as this one.

Looks-wise, the Performance Trunk isn’t the most stylish of the bunch, and it’s not meant to be. It’s available in six solid colors and two inseam options, all of which have a no-frills design. Though we have to say, because of its barebones aesthetics, the Performance Trunk looks a lot cleaner than your typical bulky hiking short. Proof markets this as a multipurpose trunk that doubles as a gym short, and after putting it through the paces, we agree with that claim. While it’s not as breathable as Ten Thousand’s award-winning Interval Short, Proof’s stretchy fabric moves easily through all sorts of workouts.

For your next vacation, you need a dressy swim trunk that can look great at the beach, bar, or restaurant. Enter this one from Bather, which has a plaid-like pattern with complementing tones of maroon and blue. This makes it more polished than your typical swim shorts, so you can easily pair it with a simple polo or the brand’s matching camp shirt and sandals for a foolproof, lunchtime fit in your arsenal. I’ve worn it to a five-star hotel’s restaurant and then straight to the pool for a mid-day cocktail.

The waistband rests gently on the waist, but you can adjust the fit using the drawstrings. Unlike the scratchy net-like liners in cheaper swim trunks, the nylon-spandex blend is silky and irritation-free, so I could wear it for hours and be comfortable. Plus, the 5.5-inch inseam flatters tall and short guys alike. In terms of functionality, the pockets are super deep for your sunglasses or wallet, while one in the back snaps shut for extra security.

Fair Harbor offers so many great trunks, we couldn't help but to feature the Anchor Short, too. It's available in a variety of solids and prints, but we're big fans of the red stripe option, since it has a cool ‘80s vibe that pairs well with laidback T-shirts, tank tops, and more. This style also comes with the BreezeKnit liner—Fair Harbor's soft, supportive alternative to traditional mesh liner found in swimwear. It provides a snug and comfortable feel, while keeping you cool as ice.

Unlike other picks on this list, The Anchor Trunk is available in three inseams. The 6-inch inseam is great for guys who want to show off their hard-earned leg gains this summer. The 8-inch inseam hits the sweet spot between casual and polished, a safe bet if you're unsure which to go for. And the 10-inch inseam is the longest option, ideal for those who prefer a bit more coverage. Plus, its relaxed (not baggy) fit keeps this from veering into Reno 911! territory.

Ralph Lauren’s classic style is the most versatile of the bunch—it works as a swim trunk, a casual short, and even a workout short. I’ve worn it for early runs along the boardwalk, knocked out quick leg circuits on vacation, and then jumped straight into the water without needing to change. The 5.75-inch inseam gives it a shorter, more athletic cut that feels mobile and unrestrictive, especially when you’re moving. But its trimmer, more style-forward look makes it clean enough to pass as an everyday short.

It’s made from a lightweight polyester-elastane blend that dries quickly and has just enough stretch that you can bend down or twist at any turn. The mesh liner feels breathable and supportive, unlike traditional swim trunks. And because the silhouette is sleek and the design is minimal—save for that signature embroidered pony—it pairs easily with a tee or button-down, making it a true wear-all-day option for beach trips, resort hangs, or any summer day that doesn’t stick to one lane.

Sure, this is the most expensive option on our list, but we think Orlebar Brown's Bulldog Trunk packs serious style that’s worthy of its higher price point. The polyester-elastane fabric is designed with a textured stripe pattern, which is very sharp and unexpected—something most guys would shy away from, so you’ll probably be the only guy at the pool rocking it. I wore mine to the beach in Costa Rica, and no one had anything remotely close to this pair; only baggy boardshort-like types.

This one lands mid-thigh at a 6-inch inseam, which is the golden length for most men. It’s not as stretchy as the Rhone pair, but you’re not rocking this for comfort; the Bulldog is all about quiet luxury. When I’m not in the water, I wear it with just about any solid polo or camp shirt for a five-star resort fit. Plus, the gold fasteners on the waist add extra style points, and just look super plush.

If you’re looking for something more surf-specific, the California-based brand best known for its laid-back, surfer aesthetic delivers the Cruise Boardshort. It’s cut from a lightweight polyester-elastane blend that dries impressively fast, making it ideal for long days in and out of the water. The scalloped leg—where the hem curves slightly upward at the outer thigh—gives you a greater range of motion and a subtle retro-sport look. It’s one of those design tweaks that looks cool but also improves mobility.

It’s built to stay put. The double drawcord waistband adds extra security, so even when you’re getting knocked around by waves or caught in a strong current, you’re not worrying about your trunks slipping. It’s a small but clutch feature if you’re actually swimming, surfing, or doing anything more active than lounging.

Patagonia used a creative, cloudy effect across its lengthy boardshort that looks like sun-faded tie-dye you’d find on a well-traveled surf trip. It’s stylish enough to stand out amongst a sea of guys in basic trunks. Like Vuori’s option, this pair falls right above the knee, providing more coverage in case your shorts ride up as you get out of the water. I’m 5'8" and the 18-inch outseam fits pretty long on me, so the fit is more flattering for taller guys around 6’ feet. If you want things even longer, the brand also offers a 21-inch version that hovers over the knee.

Despite the trimmer leg, the cut never feels restrictive—it’s got enough room to move without baggy. It’s made with a lightweight polyester-spandex fabric that has a slightly sturdier structure than most of the swim trunks, but flexes whether you’re paddling out or diving a volleyball. I found it dries down quickly between dips, and the double looped waistband ensures it stays secure.

Patagonia’s Baggies Short has proven its durability on everything from multi-day hikes to back-to-back swimming sessions. Our guys have worn this trunk for backpacking through rugged trails, jumping into rivers, and air-drying in the sun—day after day—without wearing them out. The tough nylon fabric resists abrasion from rocks, sand, and saltwater, while the water-repellent coating helps it dry quickly.

Unlike traditional slanted pockets that can balloon up in the water, this pair has mesh openings inside, so the water flows out and doesn’t weigh you down. Despite dozens of uses and hours in the ocean, we haven’t seen these trunks fade, fray, or lose shape. Our fashion editors found they pair nicely with a string of T-shirts and sneakers for coffee pick-ups or quick grocery runs.

Sometimes, swim trunks with loud designs can be too in-your-face, but Mack Weldon went easy on this pair. It’s decked out with a dotted gradient pattern that adds a visual pop, without looking tacky. Our editors have worn this with a white polo to balance things out, and works as a foolproof fit for beachside lunches. We especially like the fit: it’s relaxed, but slimmer than the Bather swim trunks for a dressier vibe.

Like most of the swim trunks on this list, this version is made with a polyester-spandex flex that offers a lot of mobility. We could maintain a full range of motion through running, squatting and walking. During our testing, the mesh liner didn’t irritate our family jewels either. In fact, it was one of the more comfortable liners on the list.

If you're performing heavy activities in swimsuits, consider doing without liners. Board shorts, for instance, aren't made with liners for more wiggle room and comfort. Otherwise, liners should fit snugly, but should never be too tight or pinch your skin. Built-in mesh liners provide extra support to your down-there and keep everything in place as you move. Since they're mesh, they're also breathable and quick-drying.

Shorts are getting shorter, and so are swim trunks. But you don't have to chase trends. Typically, around 7 inches in inseam length is a universally flattering fit that hits a couple inches above the thigh. Though if you want to show off your quads, you can't go wrong with a 5-inch inseam, either. If you're looking at outseam lengths, 14-inch is more fashion-forward, 17-inch is a casual mid-length, and 20-inch is as full coverage as you can get without wearing pants.

If you're swimming, surfing, or just moving around a lot in bathing suits, Stafford advises stretch as a must-have factor, since it allows a great fit and range of motion in and out of the water.

Waistbands should fit comfortably on your body, and a drawstring may help you to adjust the waist size if needed. We recommend a full-elastic waistband with a drawcord because it ensures you keep your trunks on, even if you’re diving from the high dive. If you’re going for a snap closure, just make sure it’s not too tight with the ability to unsnap after one beer.

To find the best swim trunks for men, our style team, led by Style and Reviews Director Christian Gollayan, spent the past five years testing more than 100 swim trunks in a variety of settings. For instance, we went whitewater rafting in Proof's performance shorts, swam laps in Rhone's R&R short, and sported Orlebar Brown swim trunks at the beach. From there, we evaluated these swim trunks based on their style, fit, price point, performance in and out of the water, and versatility to make the final top picks.

Bonus points if they doubled as athletic shorts or hiking shorts. Specifically, we looked for styles that are not trendy or trend-chasing; instead, our picks of the best swim trunks have a flattering fit, provide a ton of value at reasonable price points, and come in styles that will look great years from now.

The 12 best swim trunks for men tested by our style editors below are all quick-drying, comfortable, and look great. Whether you're planning to wear these to the beach, poolside, or to your weekend lunch, the best swim trunks for men have you covered.

Obviously, cotton is a no-go—it gets way too heavy when met with water. Our fashion team recommends lightweight synthetic fabrics since they dry quickly and are usually fade-resistant to the sun and chlorine. These synthetic fabrics are ideal for underwater wear because they won't get hefty and pull you down when drenched in water.

Poly with the addition of stretch (Lycra, elastane) material is the most common with good reason—more comfortable, forgiving. Some brands use nylon instead of poly because it absorbs less water and does not get heavy as the waterproofing wears off over time, but nylon does not (take color) dye as deeply and vibrantly as poly.

Choosing the right color and print for men's swim trunks boils down to personal style, skin tone, body type, and occasion. If you prefer a more classic and versatile look, solid colors like navy, blue, or olive are excellent choices that can easily transition from the beach to casual outings. If you want something a bit more eye-catching, subtle patterns like stripes or small geometric prints add personality without being too loud. For those who like to stand out, bright colors, tropical florals, or retro-inspired prints make a strong statement, especially on an island.

Your skin tone can also guide your choice—fairer skin tones tend to work best with earth tones and pastels, while medium to tan skin pairs well with rich blues, corals, or reds. Darker tones can pull off almost any color, but bright neons and strong contrasts pop especially well. Body type plays a factor too—shorter guys might benefit from vertical stripes or a shorter inseam to create a lengthening effect, while taller guys can balance their frame with medium-to-long inseams and bold patterns. Athletic builds look great in solid colors and tailored fits that highlight their shape, whereas stockier builds might find that darker shades or micro-prints create a more streamlined look.

Lastly, think about where you'll be wearing swim trunks. For pool parties and resort vacations, playful prints and vibrant hues add to the fun, while sporty designs with color-blocking work well for active beach days. If you're looking for swim trunks that can double as everyday shorts, muted tones or subtle patterns offer the most versatility. Ultimately, the best choice is whatever makes you feel confident and comfortable.

#4916. Rep. Raskin probes Trump Jr.’s firm’s investments into Polymarket and Juul over ‘suspiciously well-timed government favors’

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Rep. Raskin is set to lead the House Judiciary Committee next year after Democrats took both the House and Senate in the midterm elections.

Rep. Jamie Raskin, who is poised to lead a key committee next year, has expanded his investigation into Donald Trump Jr.’s venture capital firm, probing potential links between investments in companies such as Polymarket and what he has called "suspiciously well-timed government favors."

On Monday, the Maryland Democrat sent a letter to AI-focused manufacturing company Hadrian Automation, e-cigarette company Juul Labs, prediction markets platform Polymarket and online firearms retailer GrabAGun over their ties to Trump's 1789 Capital and whether President Donald Trump's son and business partners helped influence regulatory decisions or use certain knowledge to inform their investment decisions.

"The House Judiciary Committee is committed to ensuring that public officials and their families cannot profit from and exploit inside government information and insider access. The American people demand and expect nothing less," Raskin said on Tuesday.

Raskin is set to lead the House Judiciary Committee next year after Democrats took both the House and Senate in the midterm elections. Raskin first began probing 1789 Capital in August. He has also criticized President Trump's ties to the crypto industry and said he had "turned the Oval Office into the world’s most corrupt crypto startup operation."

In his letter to Polymarket on Monday, Raskin noted the Commodity Futures Trading Commission's previous settlement with Polymarket after the platform allegedly offered illicit binary options contracts and, as part of that agreement, said it would pay a $1.4 million fine, wind down its non-compliant markets and take preventive measures to block U.S. users. In July, the CFTC and the Justice Department dropped that probe last year.

Polymarket then secured an investment from 1789 Capital about a month afterwards.

"Only after the Trump Administration had cleared the way for Polymarket to compete in the U.S. market did the company disclose that 1789 Capital reportedly invested tens of millions of dollars in the company," Raskin said.

Polymarket did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Donald Trump Jr. pushed back against conflicts of interest at an Axios event, saying "the notion that I should somehow sit in a corner, suck my thumb, and wait, you know, 8 years, in this case, 12 years, 'til we're out of the political game, it's not real."

Lawmakers and policymakers at the state level have also raised concerns about other aspects of prediction markets, including insider trading and whether states or the CFTC should regulate the platforms. On Tuesday, New York City Council Speaker Julie Menin requested that prediction market executives appear before a council oversight hearing next month following an investigation alleging platforms are targeting younger people with "deceptive, predatory marketing practices."

"As a mom and consumer protection attorney, I believe we must end these companies’ predatory marketing tactics," Menin said. 'The City Council will hold a hearing to consider first-in-the-nation regulations of these practices, and we expect these companies to testify.”

#4913. Why Nvidia Needed Jensen Huang Back—And What His Return Really Changes

Why Nvidia Needed Jensen Huang Back—And What His Return Really Changes

Jensen Huang did not unveil a breakthrough chip on Monday. He simply got his old job back—and Nvidia shares surged more than 30%, while the Nasdaq jumped nearly 7% in midday trading.

The reaction revealed something more consequential than Wall Street’s fondness for a charismatic CEO: investors had spent 11 days confronting how much of the AI boom runs through one person.

Huang, who resigned November 12 amid revelations about extramarital affairs and an unproven Chinese espionage allegation involving a former customer executive, has returned as CEO, president, and director.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Nvidia with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Nvidia’s board says his reinstatement poses no national security risk, based partly on input from senior U.S. officials. Its independent investigation, however, is not over.

The company did not stop making chips. CFO Colette Kress became interim CEO, Harvey Jones took over as Nvidia’s first board chairman, and executives insisted the technology roadmap and customer commitments remained intact.

Kress presided over earnings that showed roughly $116 billion in quarterly sales and projected about $130 billion for the following quarter—staggering numbers that still failed to calm the market.

Inside Nvidia, the transition looked considerably less routine. During the first companywide meeting without Huang, staff had submitted about 700 questions, many concerning compensation and board transparency.

Kress promised pay increases and extra stock awards in December, months earlier than planned. Executives took turns assuring workers that the company could serve customers without Huang; some employees nevertheless discussed leaving if his absence became permanent.

The pushback extended outside headquarters. An online petition gathered roughly 200,000 names, though signers’ claimed employee or shareholder affiliations were not independently verified. Altimeter Capital’s Brad Gerstner and Atreides Management’s Gavin Baker, whose firms had disclosed about $2.1 billion in Nvidia shares, urged a deliberate succession process that kept Huang’s return possible. It was an unusual message to directors searching for a replacement: don't rush to replace the founder.

This letter was sent to Nvidia's board today.

Absolutely share @altcap belief that this letter outlines minimum acceptable standards. pic.twitter.com/ST2f5TMAa1

— Gavin Baker (@GavinSBaker) November 14, 2026

In other words, Nvidia couldn’t survive the scandal—not the one about Huang cheating, but the one about Huang leaving.

Huang co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and turned graphics processors into the hardware behind modern AI. But his power also lies in the relationships surrounding those chips: cloud giants buying entire data centers of GPUs, developers locked into Nvidia’s CUDA software, suppliers building its next systems and policymakers deciding which markets it can serve.

Reuters reports that he has overseen roughly 60 direct reports and personally shaped major deals and product decisions.

He has also become a financier and ambassador for the AI ecosystem. Nvidia has invested in companies including CoreWeave and Nebius, which buy computing hardware to rent AI capacity to others.

That web of investment, sales, and financing is precisely why his disappearance unnerved markets far beyond Nvidia. After his outsting, Chinese customers delayed or canceled orders while leadership was unsettled; the whole Nasdaq index and the wider S&P500 crashed and odds for a bubble pop spiked to more than 70% on prediction markets.

Then, on Monday, minutes after his return, CoreWeave and Nebius shares each jumped more than 20% alongside Nvidia which had its best day in history—Huang also probably had his best day ever, too.

To have a grasp of Huang’s influence, one just needs to follow the money.

Elon Musk backed the reinstatement campaign; Y Combinator chief Garry Tan and Epic Games’ Tim Sweeney were among other prominent supporters. Bill Ackman celebrated Monday with “sanity prevails.” Trump pressed the case personally, later saying his intervention was “very important” and that the administration assured directors there was no national security obstacle to Huang’s return.

That backing helps explain the whiplash: On November 17, Jones said the board would not reinstate Huang, citing conduct inconsistent with Nvidia’s standards. Six days later, the same directors voted unanimously to bring him back. They say meetings satisfied them about his candor and leadership, but have not explained exactly what changed. Supporters see recognition of an indispensable founder; critics see a board yielding to powerful outsiders.

Bullish analysts argue that restoring Huang reduces execution and talent-retention risk. Dan Ives has framed Nvidia as a $300 stock with Huang versus $225 without him, while Melius Research upgraded the shares to “buy” with a $220 target.

Yet Monday’s roughly 30%-plus rebound followed a Friday close of $125.53; even near $165, Nvidia remains well below its October peak. Buying the comeback means paying much more for the same projected chip sales.

The other side is harder to price. Michael Burry criticized the reversal, and legal scrutiny of Huang, Nvidia’s export controls and its board continues. The company is establishing a board committee for national security and export-control oversight and recruiting a chief compliance officer.

None of that resolves the fundamental AI question: whether customers can earn enough on massive infrastructure spending to sustain Nvidia’s growth.

Huang’s return repairs a missing link between Nvidia, its customers, Wall Street, and Washington. The 11-day crisis showed how much confidence that link carries—and how vulnerable the AI trade becomes when it breaks.

What he has not yet won back is an unquestioned mandate to lead.

#4912. Payward’s Krak plans early 2027 launch of US credit cards with bitcoin cashback, including one secured by USDC

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Two Visa Signature cards, managed by Cardless, are expected to ship in early 2027 and offer up to 4% cashback.

Kraken parent Payward's money app Krak has opened a waitlist for two U.S. credit cards, including one that lets customers use USDC as collateral while earning yield, according to a statement shared exclusively with The Block.

The Krak Credit Card and Krak Secured Card are expected to begin shipping in early 2027. Both offer up to 4% cashback in either dollars or bitcoin once a purchase settles, with rewards paid directly into customers' Krak accounts rather than issued as points.

Payward considers the cashback as part of its wider relationship with customers rather than treating the cards as standalone products, a company spokesperson told The Block. The company earns revenue from card payments, assets customers hold with it, and other everyday uses of Krak, they said.

A national survey of 2,001 U.S. adults commissioned by Krak and conducted by Morning Consult in July found that 30% of Americans do not own a credit card. Nearly two-thirds said they were frustrated with existing credit card rewards programs, while 57% preferred cash rewards over points.

"Credit is fundamental to financial opportunity in America, yet the credit card industry has spent decades pouring resources into increasingly complex rewards schemes instead of focusing on making credit products more accessible," Payward co-CEO Arjun Sethi said on Tuesday. "The result is a system that remains outdated, fragmented and out of reach for millions of people."

Krak is offering the standard card to eligible customers with established credit histories. The secured card is designed for people building or rebuilding their credit, including crypto-native customers whose wealth is primarily held outside the traditional banking system, the firm said. Krak does not guarantee that using the card will establish or improve a customer's credit score.

For the secured card, users can deposit between $350 and $5,000 in USDC, depending on their desired credit limit, the spokesperson said. The USDC remains in their Krak accounts as collateral and can earn up to 4% APY through Krakpass, while cardholders can also receive up to 4% cashback on purchases, according to the firm.

The cards also include access to Visa Signature experiences, protections, and other benefits that are offered via the Visa network, the firm said.

Krak's card lineup already includes a U.S. debit card, alongside personal accounts for cash and digital assets in the same app. Payward introduced its U.S. Krak Debit Cards earlier this year, following a UK and EU rollout of Krak debit cards, salary deposits, and new wealth tools that began in November 2025. It had launched the Krak app five months earlier in June 2025 as a rival to Venmo and PayPal. The company said Tuesday that its debit-card offering has surpassed 200,000 cardholders globally.

Payward previously disclosed plans to test a U.S. credit card in its third-quarter update earlier this month, alongside plans to settle U.S. dollar wires through its Federal Reserve master account and to fund lending in Europe with customer deposits. Payward reported $60 million in Q3 adjusted EBITDA, up from $23 million in the second quarter, but down from the $178.6 million it posted a year earlier.

Both of the U.S. credit cards require Krakpass, which costs $6.29 per month when billed annually or is free for customers with more than $3,000 in monthly direct deposits, the firm said. Payward has not yet disclosed the cards' APRs or fees, with full program terms due before issuance.

First Electronic Bank will issue the cards under a license from Visa U.S.A. The cards will be offered through Kraken and Cardless. Cardless, a fintech that provides technology and servicing for branded credit card programs, will also handle program management, technology, and servicing.

Payward said the cards will not be available in all U.S. states and that it plans to introduce similar offerings in additional markets globally over the following year.

#4908. SEC staff gives Molecule no-action relief for scientific research tokens

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

On Tuesday, the SEC's Division of Corporation Finance said it would not recommend charges to the Commission regarding Molecule's project tokens.

The Securities and Exchange Commission's staff said it would not recommend an enforcement action against decentralized science infrastructure company Molecule for its utility tokens that allow people to support scientific research.

On Tuesday, the SEC's Division of Corporation Finance said it would not recommend charges to the Commission regarding Molecule's project tokens.

"Based on the facts presented, the Division will not recommend enforcement action to the Commission if, in reliance on your opinion as counsel, LabsCo offers and sells Project Tokens in the manner and under the circumstances described in your letter without registration under Section 5 of the Securities Act and does not register Project Tokens as a class of equity securities under Section 12(g) of the Exchange Act," the SEC said in a statement.

This comes after lawyers representing Molecule sent a letter to the SEC's Division of Corporation Finance last week asking for relief. The agency has issued several crypto-related no-action letters over the past year amid its friendlier regulatory stance, including for DoubleZero, Fuse and more recently, Canton.

In the letter, the lawyers refer to a coin-to-company model, Molecule's framework that allows users to receive tokens and then gain equity ownership in science projects they support. They refer to the Howey Test — a 1946 U.S. Supreme Court case to determine if an asset qualifies as an investment contract and, therefore, a security — and say the token fails to satisfy it in part because it does not have an expectation of profit derived from the effort of others.

Molecule said its first project in the ecosystem will use coin-to-company in the next few weeks.

"The Coin-to-Company No Action Letter brings together the best of blockchains with traditional equity structures to accelerate the production and commercialization of science in the US, Europe and worldwide," said Molecule Chief Legal Officer Benjamin Snipes, in a post from the company on X.
