#4906. North Korea Moves $247 Million of Binance Hack Loot Through Bitcoin Mixers

North Korea Moves $247 Million of Binance Hack Loot Through Bitcoin Mixers

North Korea’s hackers have now funneled at least $247 million from the record $6.4 billion Binance theft through Bitcoin “coinjoins,” a mixing technique designed to sever the trail between stolen funds and their origin, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said.

The figure, roughly a month after the Oct. 29 hack, reflects just the funds pushed into coinjoins, which pool many users’ Bitcoin into a single transaction so the coins can’t be traced.

Notably, almost all of it was coordinated by a single known individual: the operator of Kruw, the most widely used coinjoin coordinator for Wasabi Wallet users. That operator “refuses to block funds such as these,” Elliptic co-founder Tom Robinson told Decrypt in an email, which is why the firm expects the laundering to continue.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Bitcoin with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

The mixing accelerated sharply in mid-November. On Nov. 17, the attacker emptied five of the nine largest Ethereum addresses tied to the theft, shifting about 488,000 ETH ($860 million) into fresh batches.

Those are being steadily swapped into Bitcoin through the Thorchain bridge and then sent into coinjoins, often within hours, the same playbook North Korea used after the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in early 2025.

The operation has spanned more than 27,000 wallets across at least 16 blockchains, roughly 55 services and some 90,000 transactions. Elliptic stressed it is clearly automated but found no evidence AI agents are directing it, pointing instead to conventional software, with fixed timing, set amounts and pauses that resemble human working patterns in Pyongyang.

Elliptic earlier found the hackers even minted their own worthless meme coins to shuffle funds through liquidity pools, one of several obfuscation tactics stacked together alongside cross-chain bridges, asset swaps and lending-vault hops.

The FBI has attributed the theft to the North Korean group TraderTraitor, and Binance has said it will cover all customer losses.

Still, little has actually been cashed out. About $2.85 billion has been frozen, and roughly $2.35 billion remains parked in theft-linked addresses, including the 18,270 BTC taken directly from Binance, which hasn’t moved since the night of the hack.

Elliptic expects the funds to be converted to Bitcoin, mixed and eventually cashed out through over-the-counter brokers over a period of months, the point at which they can still be intercepted.

With the Kruw operator declining to block the flows, though, the mixing shows little sign of slowing.

#4901. Why One Math Proof Has AI Experts Warning Humans Might Soon Lose Control

Why One Math Proof Has AI Experts Warning Humans Might Soon Lose Control

Meta Chief AI Officer Alexandr Wang warned Sunday that artificial superintelligence is approaching—and that humanity may be unable to control systems far smarter than itself.

In an essay on X titled “Superintelligence is Arriving,” Wang said the next year could determine how people live alongside machines capable of improving themselves. His catalyst was an extraordinary mathematics result involving Anthropic’s Claude.

“Superintelligence is arriving. How humanity acts over the next year will have great consequence on what future world we live in,” Wang wrote.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Wang called the proof of the Riemann Hypothesis a greater intellectual achievement than building the atomic bomb, which he described as a “homework problem” by comparison. The breakthrough, he wrote, came with help from an “alien mind that we don’t understand.”

The warning carries extra weight because Wang runs Meta Superintelligence Labs. Mark Zuckerberg brought him aboard in 2025 after Meta invested $14.3 billion for a 49% stake in Wang’s startup, Scale AI.

On Friday, Anthropic mathematician Levent Alpöge said he and Claude had proved the Riemann Hypothesis, a conjecture about prime numbers that had resisted mathematicians since 1859. The claim arrived a day after Anthropic’s massive IPO.

Alpöge released roughly 17 million lines of code in Lean, a system that checks mathematical proofs. On Saturday, the Lean and Mathlib communities certified that the code proves the correct statement; researchers are still working to explain its mathematics in conventional form.

For Wang, the result suggests raw reasoning power is no longer AI’s central limitation. He argued that the remaining hurdles involve specialist knowledge, experimentation, and the tools needed to apply intelligence in other fields.

That includes AI research itself. If machines can redesign more capable successors, Wang fears a cycle of recursive self-improvement could move faster than human monitoring or intervention.

“It is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement,” he wrote. He also questioned human control over hypothetical systems “1000x smarter” than people.

Wang criticized the industry’s “lazy thinking” that superintelligence is inevitable and the only question is making it safe. “We should not be okay with a ‘handover’ of society to AI,” he wrote, advocating technology that strengthens human decision-making.

In a Monday follow-up, he said Meta had delayed its Muse launch for months over safety concerns and backed industry safeguards. Zuckerberg, he added, wants most computing power devoted to serving customers rather than unrestricted self-improvement.

Meta nevertheless has its own ambitions. Its Muse Wonder models, released in October, compete with systems from OpenAI and Anthropic, making Wang both a critic of the race’s direction and a leading participant.

Not everyone accepts his leap from mathematics to superintelligence. NYU professor Andrew Gordon Wilson argued that proving an existing conjecture is different from inventing new mathematical frameworks such as calculus or relativity.

“Nothing compares to proving the Riemann Hypothesis in terms of sheer intellectual might”. I disagree. The introduction of calculus, general relativity, and quantum mechanics, are more profound intellectual achievements. Creativity, taste, and intuition is the difference.

— Andrew Gordon Wilson (@andrewgwils) November 23, 2026

The Riemann result demonstrates a remarkable human-AI collaboration, but it does not prove that autonomous superintelligence exists.

Wang has urged the industry to change course while Meta continues developing the systems he fears could eventually exceed human oversight.

#4898. Sky lender proposes buyback of up to $50M to fund stUSDS withdrawals

Borrowers would sell SKY to the protocol and use the proceeds to pay down their loans, freeing up funds for depositors in the stUSDS pool.

A lender in Sky Protocol’s stUSDS pool proposed Monday that the project spend $40 million to $50 million of its reserves buying SKY tokens from borrowers to free up funds for depositors who have struggled to withdraw.

Depositors lend USDS, Sky’s dollar-pegged stablecoin, to borrowers who put up staked SKY as collateral. The pool has been at or near full utilization since Nov. 10, leaving depositors reliant on new deposits or loan repayments to withdraw.

The lender, who posts as Conquercube, called for a vote on an over-the-counter buyback. Each sale would use the USDS proceeds to pay down the seller’s loan in the same transaction. Conquercube estimated the buyback could bring utilization down to between 68% and 74%.

Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery found that a wallet linked to Sky co-founder Rune Christensen accounted for 48% of the debt backed by the pool as of Thursday.

Christensen acknowledged Monday that his borrowing contributed to depositors’ difficulty withdrawing. He said he repaid 3 million USDS and still owed about 77 million USDS on loans backed by his staked SKY. To repay the balance, he said he was exploring a direct sale of some of his SKY tokens to institutional investors.

#4894. I Tried Adding 10 Minutes of Daily Mobility to My Routine for Two Weeks. Here’s How My Body—And Mind—Changed

Plus, the expert-backed reason a few minutes of mobility can add up over time.

I’m in my early 30s, which means staying fit requires more of a conscious, consistent effort. The natural loss of lean muscle mass and skin elasticity that occurs at this stage of life makes me more mindful of my body and overall well-being. So, I’ve declared this my year of wellness. As part of it, I have been working on adding small bouts of movement that manageably fit into my daily life. Mobility work felt like one of the best ways to do that.

Mobility is an incredibly underrated training modality, yet it’s an essential part of healthy aging—especially for those like me who spend most of the day sitting at a desk. Mobility training focuses on controlled, active movement through a joint’s full range of motion, says Matthew Cifelli, PT, DPT, CSCS, of Attain Physical Therapy.

This style of movement helps you perform nearly every daily task, from climbing stairs and getting up from a seated position to walking with a full stride and turning your head while driving. But as you age or spend extended periods of time being sedentary, it’s common for muscles to tighten, joints to stiffen, and movement to become less efficient.

“Limited mobility forces the body to compensate with inefficient patterns, increasing the risk of pain, balance issues, and injury,” says Cifelli. Maintaining mobility, on the other hand, “supports independence, joint health, and the ability to move confidently through life.”

That’s why I recently decided to add just 10 minutes of mobility work to my daily routine for two weeks. Here’s exactly what I did—and how my body changed.

Mobility is “not just about ‘feeling loose,’” says Cifelli. “It's about teaching the body to use its flexibility in real movement.” It’s a mix of static stretching and flexibility work that focuses on muscle length. “Mobility training targets how well a joint moves and how well you control that motion, which requires coordinated muscle activation and joint stability.”

And just a few minutes a day of mobility work can help boost circulation, lubricate the joints, alleviate muscle tension, and improve confidence in daily movement. “Even short sessions, when done daily, create meaningful cumulative change,” he says.

My routine included a combination of exercises and hold times I learned from Portia Page, CPT, in addition to a couple of stretches my physical therapist recommended incorporating into my home regimen. I performed each exercise for one minute, one time through.

During the first week of my experiment, I eased into my 10-minute mobility routine. I was eager to see how it could help improve my “desk body,” a.k.a. tight hips, tense hamstrings, and lower back and neck tension.

I did the workout every morning around 8 a.m. before diving into the madness of the day. It was pretty seamless to add to my mornings, since I have a dedicated workout space in my home, though the routine doesn’t require much space or equipment. Plus, I’m someone who craves routine. Sticking to the same time each morning made this fitness habit sustainable.

From the get-go, it was clear my body was tense and in desperate need of some stretching and mobility work. My legs, back, and hips all felt stiff at the beginning of the workout. Certain exercises, like the standing forward fold, hip flexor stretch, and supine figure-four stretch were more challenging than the rest. For example, during the first week, I found it difficult to touch the floor when doing the forward fold, so I didn’t force it.

By the end of each session, my body was definitely a bit more relaxed, but I knew I needed to stick with mobility work for more than just a week or two in order to achieve meaningful, lasting results.

I was a dancer growing up, and my body was much more flexible then than it is now. While the Pilates classes I attend three to four times a week have definitely helped me stretch and strengthen my body, a dedicated mobility routine felt like exactly what the doctor ordered. I didn’t realize it would help me in my classes, too. By the end of week one, I felt I was able to move through a more seamless range of motion in certain Pilates exercises like the mermaid and the straight-back hinge on the short box.

Heading into week two, I found myself looking forward to my morning mobility session. It started to feel like more of a natural segue into my day, similar to brushing my teeth, taking my vitamins, or getting dressed. Once I wrapped up stretching and sat down at my desk, my body felt more limber and relaxed as I started work. However, after a couple hours of sitting, I felt much more tense again. The experiment taught me to be mindful of that: My body needs movement throughout the day—not just first thing in the morning.

I felt inspired by this healthy mobility habit and how great I feel afterward, so I wanted to keep the momentum going. During week two, I started building other healthy habits, like getting up to walk around my kitchen and living room every hour and going on a longer brisk walk at the end of the day. Both helped me stay limber throughout the day, rather than just in the morning. My hope is that, with all of these bouts of movement combined, sitting won’t undo all of the wonderful benefits I get from my stretching routine.

By the end of the second week, I was pleased that my body felt more accustomed to the dedicated mobility work than it had during week one. The more challenging stretches also became slightly easier, which made me feel like I was making genuine progress.

The routine became a no-brainer—a staple, if you will—in my morning routine: I’d wake up, brew a soothing latte, and get to work on my yoga mat. It also sparked the desire to get up and move more during the workday rather than remain hunched over my laptop for an extended period of time.

Because I experienced the tension-relieving benefits of this mobility work almost immediately after each session during week two, I’m now inspired to do it at both the start and end of my day. It’s a great way to ease any built-up stiffness after a long day at my desk before slipping into bed.

That said, one thing I’d love to continue working on is my posture. I didn’t experience any groundbreaking improvements during this two-week timeframe, but I’m hopeful that I’ll make progress down the line. I’m also curious to see whether being consistent with this mobility routine long-term will help my body—especially my hips and lower back—feel less stiff after sitting or lounging for longer periods.

Consistency is key. As with any workout, consistency is essential for success. But alas, it’s something I’ve struggled with over the years. Hectic work weeks filled with deadlines and appointments typically push my fitness to the back burner.

However, during this two-week test period, I was incredibly determined to carve out 10 minutes each day for my stretching regimen, and I’m so glad I did. Sticking with this fitness habit helped me feel better mentally and physically. My body started to feel more flexible and limber by the end of the two-week period—something I don’t think I would have experienced if I were inconsistent with my efforts.

According to Page, a mere 5 to 10 minutes a day of mobility work can make a noticeable difference over time if you’re consistent with the habit. She recommends thinking of stretching “not as a luxury but as basic maintenance for your body—like brushing your teeth.”

Kickstarting the day with fitness can promote other healthy habits. Starting the day with mobility work inspired me to follow other healthy habits throughout. After all, I was already off to a solid start and wanted to keep that momentum going strong.

Following each mobility session, I felt accomplished, energized, and ready to tackle the day. I found myself sticking with my regular Pilates workouts, making all-around healthier meal choices, snacking less, and adding brief “movement snacks” to my afternoons to break up the time spent sitting at my desk.

Mobility is a low-barrier starting point for movement. What I love best about this mobility workout? I don’t need to leave the comfort of my home or have access to fancy equipment. I’ve seamlessly incorporated these exercises into my self-care routine, and it just takes 10 minutes of my morning before I dive into work deadlines and commitments.

While there’s always room for improvement—and I’d like to add even more stretches to my routine—I felt incredibly accomplished overall with only a 10-minute routine. I’m hopeful that, by starting now and being consistent with mobility training, I can help prepare my body to tackle everyday tasks with ease. This training can also help me stay mobile, confident, and independent in my adventurous lifestyle for the long term.

Alexa is a content strategist, editor, and writer based in Greenwich, Connecticut, who has more than 12 years of experience writing for travel, lifestyle, and fitness publications. Alexa’s work has appeared in Business Insider, Parade, Men's Journal, Apartment Therapy, Men’s Fitness, Allrecipes, Family Handyman, Eat This, Not That!, Body Network, Everyday Health, SheBuysTravel, Tasting Table, Elite Daily, and more. When she’s not writing, you’ll likely find her at Pilates, hiking, designing her next tablescape, or jetting off on a getaway.

#4892. OpenAI Will Pay You Up to $8K to Preach the AI Gospel to Small Businesses

OpenAI Will Pay You Up to $8K to Preach the AI Gospel to Small Businesses

OpenAI wants people to spread the gospel of ChatGPT to their local businesses. And it is willing to pay them up to $8,000 a month to do it.

The company launched OpenWorks, a program that recruits AI-savvy individuals and small consulting teams to help neighborhood businesses figure out what to do with the technology. The idea is to move beyond telling a restaurant owner that AI will change everything and actually build something useful for the restaurant.

OpenAI will pay selected partners a monthly stipend of up to $8,000. In exchange, applicants must commit to four full days of free, in-person work with local businesses each month, plus one community workshop. It's not an $8,000 salary just for being good at prompts.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

That said, this isn't a full-time job with an automatic $96,000 salary. The company advertises a maximum monthly stipend and asks partners to spend four full days each month providing free, in-person assistance, plus hosting one community workshop.

The application asks candidates to describe a real problem they've solved using AI, what changed afterward, and which businesses they'd help first. Knowing how to give ChatGPT clever instructions apparently isn't enough; experience in coding, automation, marketing or operations helps.

Small businesses pay nothing for the service. They pick a problem, work with a partner on a solution, and get three months of ChatGPT Business at no cost. OpenAI says the pilot covers independently owned U.S. businesses, but leaves out startups backed by venture capital, private-equity-owned companies, and software firms.

The company has some examples to sell the idea. In Fargo, North Dakota, the owners of Tiny Things, a gift shop selling miniatures, used ChatGPT with an OpenWorks partner to build an inventory dashboard. One owner estimated that commissioning a similar tool would have cost about $50,000.

In Charleston, a microgreens grower used the chatbot to find restaurants and write personalized pitches to chefs. He estimates his response rate went from 20% to 75% and says he landed 10 new restaurant customers in four months. Those are the businesses' own accounts, not independently audited results.

OpenWorks is starting in 20 communities, including New York, Chicago, Los Angeles, and Fargo. OpenAI is inviting applicants elsewhere, too, under the slogan "Help us reach every community in America."

The push comes as the company works to turn consumer enthusiasm into recurring business usage. As Decrypt reported in April, enterprise customers already accounted for more than 40% of OpenAI's revenue, according to the company.

Competition also provides an incentive. Anthropic overtook OpenAI in one measure of business AI adoption earlier this year, even as ChatGPT remained a major consumer destination.

Getting ChatGPT into more neighborhood businesses gives OpenAI another route to paying customers, though the company hasn't said what happens after the free three-month offer. It also follows an October effort with America's Small Business Development Centers to train advisers and reach at least 1,000 businesses through workshops.

OpenAI has not announced a general application deadline or a fixed stipend for everyone accepted. The form is open now, and its most important question is pretty straightforward: Can you actually show up four full days a month and help somebody run a business better?

#4890. Get the Women’s Health Beginner Workout Plan: 4-Week Strength Training and Cardio Program

It’s not just you—everyone really is starting to work out lately. My feed is full of people hitting the weights for the first time ever or setting big goals like getting their first pull-up. And let me just say: It’s awesome!

So, if that’s you, let me officially say welcome to the party. When you’re starting to work out, it can be hard to sift through all the options and find a program that’s actually accessible for a true beginner (or, someone restarting after quite some time). That is, until now.

The Women’s Health+ 4-Week Beginner Workout Plan takes the guesswork out of starting your fitness journey. This comprehensive program guides you through strength and cardio workouts that only take between 20 and 45 minutes. And, of course, it answers every burning question you could have about starting a fitness routine.

No matter your age or experience, starting an exercise routine has many benefits, says Kristen McParland, CPT, your coach for the next four weeks. Building muscle increases total-body strength; your bones, ligaments, and tendons get healthier; and even your blood pressure and immunity may improve. So lace up those sneakers and we’ll see you at the gym now, too.

Download the WH+ 4-Week Beginner Workout Plan

Over the next four weeks, you’ll follow a specific plan each day, focusing either on strength training, optional cardio, active recovery, or rest (more on these, below). Each strength workout includes a warm-up and a cooldown (which you can use on cardio days, too). More info on each below.

Simply follow the instructions of each full-body strength training workout in the Women's Health+ Beginner Workout Plan. You can find detailed how-to instructions for each exercise in the Exercise Guide section of the PDF.

Equipment Needed: Dumbbells, kettlebells, a step or box, and a slam ball. Don’t have access to a kettlebell or slam ball? Don't worry—you can still do those exercises with a dumbbell.

Cardio is important for everyone, regardless of fitness level or routine. That’s because when you’re lifting heavy, your heart needs to be able to take on that stress. Training cardio—through running, cycling, or brisk walking—will help improve your heart strength.

If you’re a beginner when it comes to cardio, simply start at your own pace and build. Complete 20 to 30 minutes of running, biking, cycling, rowing, or using the elliptical or stair climber (whatever you enjoy). No matter which form of cardio you choose, start with interval training: exert yourself for 30 seconds, then go at a light pace for 1 to 2 minutes, alternating the pattern for a total of 20 minutes. The goal is to “get your heart rate up, but then pull it back and recover,” McParland says. Eventually, you’ll be able to decrease the rest time and maintain that higher-intensity movement for 15 to 20 minutes without a break.

Your activity on this day is up to you. The goal is to do some kind of low-intensity movement lasting 20 minutes. Some ideas: outdoor walk or hike, bike ride, incline treadmill walk, yoga, or Pilates class.

Take this day to relax so your muscles have time to repair. Still, if your body is craving some type of activity for recovery, you could stretch, go for a walk, or even try a treatment like a massage, sauna, or cold-plunge session.

Here’s how things will progress each week.

Complete the five programmed workouts when it’s convenient for you. Focus on your form and take your two rest days when you need to recover.

If you completed all the workouts in Week 1, repeat them during Week 2, adjusting one variable in your routine, McParland says: the number of reps or sets, the weight, the speed of the moves (slower is harder). If you missed a workout, don’t stress. Refocus on completing all of the Week 1 workouts before making any of the changes above.

You’ve officially done two full weeks of this workout plan—nice job! During this third week, repeat the workouts from Week 2 in exactly the same way or adjust one variable again (reps or sets, weight, range of motion, speed, and rest periods). If you feel you’re crushing it and are ready to level up, try the third full-body strength workout.

Still going strong? Thought so! Hit all six workouts and adjust one variable per workout for an extra challenge (reps or sets, weight, range of motion, speed, rest time). You can also ramp up your cardio time (to 30 to 40 minutes) or speed, shorten rest intervals (for interval training), or raise your step goal by 10 percent (e.g., if your daily step goal was 5,000 a day, make it 5,500).

Ready to become your strongest self? Download the WH+ 4-Week Beginner Workout Plan now.

When you’re just beginning to work out, do a minimum of two full-body strength training days each week, McParland recommends. During both of those days, you should hit at least one exercise from each category of the foundational movement patterns. This will give you enough of a challenge to stimulate muscle growth, she says.

During the first two weeks of this plan, you will have two strength training days per week (each session will run 36 to 43 minutes), two optional cardio/step days per week (each session will 20 to 30 minutes), one day of active rest (a stint of low-intensity movement lasting at least 20 minutes), and two recovery days, for a total of five workouts per week.

After those two weeks, see how your body feels and if your body is craving a third day of full-body training. If so, progress to the next phase of this plan. That weekly schedule consists of three strength training days per week (each session will run 36 to 43 minutes), two optional cardio/step days per week (each session will run 20 to 30 minutes), one active rest day (lasting at least 20 minutes), and one recovery day, for a total of six workouts per week. If your body isn’t ready for three strength-training days yet, wait another two weeks. Once you’ve completed the full four weeks of this plan, consider adding in the third day.

Start with medium weights that will challenge you for 10 to 12 reps, says Sariya. Essentially, you’ll want to feel you’ve reached 70 to 80 percent of your rate of perceived exertion (i.e., your fatigue level is at a 7 or 8 out of 10) by the time you’re doing your final reps in a set. When you feel you can do more than 12 reps, it’s time to increase your weight, but keep the same number of reps. This way, you’re always working toward your goal of building strength.

As for how much to increase by: a good rule of thumb is 2.5 to 5 pounds for an upper-body exercise and 5 to 10 pounds for a lower-body move.

What if I need to rest in the middle of a set?

Resting during a workout is important—if you don’t take enough time to recover between exercises, you won’t have enough energy in your body for the next one. With this plan, you’ll rest between moves in a superset or tri-set for 20 to 30 seconds, or however long it takes to transition between exercises. Between supersets and tri-sets, you’ll rest for one minute.

If you get fatigued in the middle of a set, push through if you feel safe doing so, Sariya says. However, if you feel dizzy or fear you might drop a weight on yourself, put it down and take time to recover. When you’re feeling better, you can start again. If you’re in any sort of pain, stop the workout entirely.

Cardio is a great supplement to strength training, McParland says. Plus, it has a slew of benefits, such as better endurance in strength training, better blood pressure regulation, and higher VO2 max, she says.

If you’re a beginner when it comes to cardio, simply start at your own pace and build. Pick any form of cardio you enjoy, whether that’s brisk walking, running, cycling, or swimming, and sprinkle it in two or three days a week for 20 to 30 minutes, McParland recommends.

On an active rest day, you’ll still exercise a bit through low-intensity movement, like a leisurely bike ride or hike. On a recovery day, you’ll do just that: recover. After all, you need to give your muscles time to rebuild and repair, Sariya says. But to maximize the recovery aspect, you can also opt to stretch, go for a gentle walk, or even get a self-care body treatment like a massage.

Once you’ve got the WH+ Beginner Workout Plan under your belt, you’re ready to take things to another level. One way to do that is by simply restarting the program—and completing the moves with heavier weights this time. (This is called progressive overload, and it’s another foundational concept in strength training. The idea is that over time, you will keep increasing the stimulus to your muscles to continuously challenge them and make them stronger.)

Picking the right weight is an art as much as it is a science, but for a general rule of thumb, for upper-body moves, increase your weights by 2.5 to 5 pounds and see how that feels. For lower-body ones, try adding 5 to 10. You should feel like you’re ending each set two to three reps away from failure (where your form starts to break down). You can tell you’re close if your range of motion starts to shorten or your reps are slowing down. That’s how you know you’re successfully challenging yourself. And, if you don’t happen to have a lot of different-sized weights at your disposal, you can always just increase the number of reps you do instead.

But, if you’re looking for something new, WH+ has a bunch of other trainer-created programs to help you grow your knowledge and confidence in the gym and attack other fitness goals.

If you’re getting into working out from the bone density perks...

Try the WH+ 4 Weeks to Stronger Bones Plan

If you’re on a body recomposition journey...

#4889. The 10 Best Fitness Trackers, Tested by Our Fitness and Tech Experts

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

Our fitness team recommends trackers from Garmin, Apple, and Suunto.

NOT SURE WHAT fitness tracker is right for you? You've come to the right place. We've been wearing and documenting fitness trackers as far back as 2015, when we reviewed the very first Apple Watch. We started cross-comparing fitness trackers as early as 2017, when we broke down the best fitness trackers for activities like strength training, running, hiking, golf, and more. Now in 2026, we're more dialed into the state of fitness trackers than ever before as nearly all our editors are wearing some sort of device on a daily basis.

On the most basic level, the best fitness trackers offer GPS connectivity to help you stay on your training course and basic activity tracking. Newer iterations of fitness trackers include advanced smartwatches that can do anything from tracking your archery workout to detecting a heart attack, as well as (our personal favorite) smart rings. Smart rings pack a ton (no literally, an absurd amount) of sleep/stress stats and workout/health information into a tiny package you wear on your finger 24/7.

Depending on what your health and fitness goals are (like losing weight, improving your sleep, or cutting time off your mile PR), the best fitness trackers can help you identify where your training is lacking, optimize your stress levels, improve your recovery time, and streamline your training sessions and daily wellness habits.

As the Fitness and Reviews Editor at Men’s Health, it’s my job to test the latest and greatest fitness trackers. I am supported in this effort by my fellow editors like Fitness Director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S., Grooming Editor Sean Zucker, Senior Gear Editor Ryan Brower, and Senior Fitness Editor Brett Williams, NASM. We have put countless fitness trackers through the gauntlet and evaluated models on accuracy, design, durability, and price. We also paid close attention to comfort, ease of use, syncing with our favorite apps, and their ability to meet our standards for any activity.

We update our lineup of fitness trackers often, so you can expect up-to-date information that takes into account the latest fitness tracker releases.

If you want a basic level fitness tracker, the Whoop PEAK membership with its new 5.0 band may be your answer. Men’s Health Fitness Director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S. backs this device for that reason. “Simplicity is what works here, because it's reliable, has great battery life, and I don't need to think about it,” he says. The Whoop 5.0 Band is a fitness tracker band, which means though it’s still decently functional, it does not have a display like most wrist devices. It tracks all of your data in the corresponding phone app, but it’s worth noting, you cannot see the data directly on your wrist.

If you like the no-frills appearance, the Whoop 5.0 can definitely accomplish most of your tracking needs. “Whoop's activity sensing has improved over the years and continues to take steps,” says Samuel. It can track anything from running, cycling, and rowing to weightlifting, yoga, soccer, and even massage therapy. From our personal experience with Whoop, we love how it fits on the wrist. It is one of the more comfortable fitness trackers we’ve worn—the strap has a soft back, is woven with microfilament yarn, and can also be easily adjusted.

Unfortunately, Samuel had some qualms with the heart rate monitoring. “The device isn't tremendously accurate in terms of tracking heart rate, which is important to note,” he says. “In general, note that whatever [Whoop] is telling you about heart rate, it's almost always overshooting.” This can be an issue because a lot of the other tracking metrics (calories, activity, etc.) are based around heart rate. However, if you just want something basic to wear on your wrist, count your steps, and give you a general look into your health and workouts, Whoop 5.0 Band with the Peak membership will do just that.

Oura was a pioneer in the smart ring space, and it’s still the best smart ring we’ve tested to date (and we’ve tested a lot). Multiple editors have been wearing an Oura Ring for the last ten years and can attest that each new iteration outkicks the competition. This particular release updated a few physical features: Oura shifted to a fully titanium build (vs. a titanium exterior, epoxy interior), added smaller and larger sizes, and ditched its three-nodule sensor design. This latest model eliminates the nodules that used to be on the inside of the ring and pinch into my finger on the Oura Ring 3. Those aren't required anymore with the new tech, so that is a huge improvement over the past model.

The battery can also last up to eight days now, instead of just seven. And the updated software is available on all models, new and old. While Oura has always offered heart rate monitoring, the new software now features automatic heart rate monitoring, allowing you to detect and track your workout stats without hitting the “Record HR workout” button. This automatic detection feature also lends itself to workout monitoring for over 40 activities (a new capability compared to the past, solely cardio-focused tracking that Oura offered).

The daytime stress features have also gotten an improvement—one of our favorites is illness detection. The Oura Ring 5 can detect changes in your stress stats and body temperature that may indicate an illness before you feel the entirety of the symptoms. A couple of months ago, one editor felt a slight earache when they woke up, and Oura called out they may have a cold coming on. The next day, that cold had arrived. It’s not always entirely accurate like this—there have been a few times the Oura Ring has said we were getting sick when it was actually just a hangover (oops).

Even as Oura lovers, we’d be remiss not to call out one of the downsides to the product: In order to attain full use of the Oura Ring, you need to pay a monthly subscription fee of $5.99 (or $69.99 per year). There are other smart rings in the space that don’t require any fee (shout out to our second favorite product, the Ultrahuman ring), but with the updated technology and consistent top-of-the-line features, we deem the extra $69.99 per year a pretty minimal price to pay.

For the most style-conscious users, the company also released a Ceramic version of the Oura Ring 4, which offers a unique look and additional color options.

Read More: Our Hands-On Test of the Oura Ring 5

Adventurers, look this way. We’re not exaggerating when we say this is the most rugged fitness tracker we have tested. The Garmin Instinct 3 AMOLED is packed with technology and has a thick face made for trail hikes, rock climbing, and even swimming (it’s water rated up to 100 meters). According to Men’s Health contributor Korin Miller, this Garmin can take a beating. “I accidentally banged it into several doorways and even scraped it against a kettlebell during a clumsy gym moment—it still looks brand new,” Miller says.

In addition to its outwardly masculine look and durable design, the technology here is super simple to navigate, which can’t be said for all fitness trackers. Though there’s no touchscreen, Miller had the five buttons figured out within just a few minutes of receiving the product. And while you can track up to 30 different workout types, Miller praises the display for the easy-to-digest layout outside of workout mode. “The 390-by-390-pixel AMOLED display resolution is sharp and bright, allowing Garmin to jam a lot of stats on the home screen. At a glance, you can see your step count, heart rate, weather, date, time, and more. It’s arranged smartly in a way to deliver plenty of information without feeling overwhelming,” she says.

But one of our favorite features? Garmin prioritizes a wind-down time, switching from a jam-packed interface to just a clock during the time you are typically sleeping (which you note during set-up). It’s worth mentioning that in order to be this rugged and durable, the Garmin Instinct 3 is also quite heavy at 53 grams. But if you’re going hard on the trails, there’s no better wrist partner.

Read More: Our Hands-On Test of the Garmin Instinct 3

At this cost, you’d expect a simple appearance, no touchscreen, and limited functionality. However, the Google Pixel Watch 5 has a beautiful, easy-to-navigate touchscreen, a smooth design, and almost all the metrics you’d get on a more high-end device.

Google puts a big emphasis on overall wellness rather than just fitness tracking, focusing on breathing and stress management. “This is a very underrated device and it's gotten more accurate with the third edition,” says Samuel. “The breathing exercises don't reinvent the wheel, but the presentation is solid and the timing is good. Overall, in this market, there's a lot we don't fully understand about stress levels, and some of the algorithms are simplistic. But Google has smartly gotten us thinking about how we feel, and that's a good thing.”

If this watch tracks stress, readiness, and sleep, what are the limitations of this budget-friendly device? Well, it doesn’t offer as many workout modes as most other fitness trackers, clocking in at just 41 options. You also have to charge it every day, and it’s only compatible with Android devices. But for such a clean, aesthetic appearance and great individual wellness metrics, we definitely consider this fitness tracker a steal.

Most fitness trackers carry a hefty price tag, so if you’re in the market but not looking to sell out on this particular accessory, the Suunto Race S is the best value you’ll find. With a high definition AMOLED touchscreen, over 95 sport modes, and a water rating up to 50m, the specs here are just as good as you’ll find on many high-end fitness trackers. The AMOLED display on this watch is fantastic for its compact design. And personally, I think the Suunto Race S is superior to the larger model.

A lot of fitness trackers in this price range (around $350) offer only more basic tracking, like steps and calories burned. That’s not the case here, there is more to this fitness watch than just a run tracker—there are workouts and other great fitness insights, like HRV, sleep monitoring, activity tracking and race pacing. Sure, it's not as powerful as a top-of-the-line Garmin, but it gets the job done and brings great value.

Let’s chat about some of those features. Sleep tracking is having a moment and the Suunto Race S can track your sleep duration, timing, deep sleep, light sleep, heart rate, REM and HRV from sleep. One downside here? Wearing a watch to bed isn’t for everyone. Another cool feature worth digging into is race pacing. If you’re training for an upcoming marathon (or just life), the Race Pacer can help keep you on track when the mid-long run rut strikes. Overall, this is a great budget-friendly find—just note that if you opt for the titanium version over the stainless steel version, that will hike the price up by $120 (which makes it not as budget-friendly).

Apple fans already know how great the Apple Watch Ultra 4 is. It features a large, durable aerospace-grade titanium body built for outdoor adventure. It’s water-rated up to 100m and boasts dual-frequency GPS. What’s that do for you exactly? It offers much more accurate tracking in tough environments (like densely populated cities, mountain terrain, and forests)—great for trail runners and climbers.

Apple has always been at the top of the game for fitness tracking. The Ultra 4 offers all the typical metrics of the older Apple Watch models—activity rings, over 60 workout modes, blood oxygen, ECG, etc. But the Ultra 4 can be used as a dive computer, an emergency siren, and a compass that retraces your steps (ideal for those prone to getting lost mid-hike).

A watch that can do this much won’t look dainty on the wrist, though. Men’s Health Senior Fitness Editor Brett Williams, NASM wrapped everything up nicely here. “The new design overhauled the sensor, which is on the rear of the watch and sits against the wearer’s wrist. Even though the casing is still the same 49-millimeter size as previous versions of the device and the sensor takes up the same amount of space.” Williams continues, “There’s now more surface area for device-to-skin contact for ECG readings, a new, circular optical sensing array, and larger, more efficient LEDs for heart rate sensing.”

For us, the only real downside is that it’s a bit chunky on the wrist, but we’ll take it—the Apple Watch Ultra 4 is truly the full package for all iPhone users.

Read More: Our Hands-On Test of the Apple Watch Ultra 4

If you’ve ever been big into running, you’ve likely considered a Garmin watch. But the Garmin Fēnix 9 isn’t just great for run tracking—it tracks over 100 different types of workouts and can last up to 29 days on one charge. In addition to its functionality, the Garmin Fēnix 9 also boasts a high-end design. One of the thinner offerings in the way of chunky Garmins, this is the perfect fitness tracking watch for anyone who doesn’t want to wear anything too cumbersome on the wrist (plus it now comes in a 43mm size). This watch has everything that fitness tracking powerhouse Garmin has to offer packed into its low-profile casing.

One of the coolest offerings? Garmin has both an AMOLED and a solar-powered option. The solar-powered watch can extend the battery life even further, but the screen is not quite as bright as the AMOLED option. The solar watch also does not have a touchscreen like the AMOLED one, making it a bit less convenient to have to click through the features via buttons.

While the Garmin Fēnix 9 has the capabilities to track pretty much any metric you want tracked, the charging experience falls a bit short. While the battery can last up to 24 days in low-power mode, turning on GPS drops that to just under 24 hours of charge—a stark difference. Additionally, the charging cord is a bit short, so you might want to keep these USB-C charger extension cords on hand to charge your Garmin.

Overall, this is a sleek, all-in-one package with a unique (and modern) solar-powered option. If you don’t mind a bit of a sub-par charging experience, you can’t get more premium than the Garmin Fēnix 9.

No iPhone? No problem. The Galaxy Watch9 can go anywhere thanks to a large scratch-resistant screen and a strong titanium case. One of our favorite features is the advanced sleep coaching that analyzes your sleep habits, suggests what you can do to improve, and is even FDA-approved to detect sleep apnea. We also appreciate the body composition reader—in just a few seconds, the Galaxy Watch9 works to tell you your vitals, and even provides a body fat estimate. These stats are prone to inaccuracy (few home devices can accurately track body fat), but we give props to Samsung for trying to push metrics to another level.

The Galaxy Watch9 also delivers the features you expect in high-end smartwatches. That means accurate dual-frequency GPS specs and voice navigation. Nailing these (as Samsung does) is extra-critical, partly because the Galaxy Watch9 has some new competition for Android users (namely, from Google's Pixel Watch).

Thanks to several generations of trial and error and new AI health insights, the Galaxy Watch gets the edge as our preferred pick for Android owners. There are more health insights and data in this watch than most competitors. Once you grant the device access to your health insights on your phone, it will make recommendations on sleep, exercise, and overall health, after you choose what your own personal goals are. I appreciate the design as well, which looks more like a classic watch than other smartwatch options.

The array of features available and the materials used to craft the watch have been optimized a few times over, thus producing a seriously strong, long-lasting fitness tracker (and everyday smart watch) most users will be happy adding to their training arsenal.

When you’re just starting out, you probably want a no-fuss fitness tracker to help get your bearings. The G-Shock Move is easy to navigate and offers just enough tracking to get you started. Men’s Health Grooming and Commerce Editor Sean Zucker tested it out for himself and likes how easy it is for newbies. “Thankfully, where the watch really excels is where it’s most meant to be used—when you’re working out or going for a run,” Zucker says. “Its face has a simple design and navigating it was a breeze, even for someone as technologically challenged as myself. It was easy to switch between lap/route times, heart rate/zone, homepage, etc. while I was running with only a couple of clicks (the watch itself only has 4 buttons so I appreciate the simplified design).”

The G-Shock Move tracks all your basic needs: steps, running, walking, heart rate, blood oxygen, and even sleep recovery. Zucker also found it quite comfortable. “[It’s a] super comfortable watch, and handles sweat/rain with ease,” he says. “It doesn’t move around at all or get greasy after a long run/workout.” Though he did have a couple qualms with the functionality.

“Now, I’m not someone who likes to wear a watch, let alone a fitness watch, all of the time but I will if I have a few minutes or I’m planning out a run,” says Zucker. “I’ll check in on what I’ve done that week and unfortunately, the app doesn’t let you access any of that info unless your phone is currently connected, which is, in my professional opinion, quite annoying.” Another small downside, Zucker did mention he had a tough time with initial setup.

“It’s important to note that I am generally 100 years old and grumpy about this type of stuff, [but] still, the set up on this watch was a bit of a pain,” says Zucker. “There were a lot of ads (this extends to the app itself after you start using as well, very muddy).” If you can get past the setup and are okay with only being able to check past stats when you’re connected to the phone app, this is a great beginner-friendly tracker offering all the basic functions you need to kick off your fitness tracking journey.

Fitbit is back, with a vengeance. The Fitbit Air is the brand’s smallest and lightest tracker ever, weighing just 12 grams and featuring a design 25 percent smaller than the discontinued Luxe. Setup is simple through the new Google Health app, and the Air’s tracking features, including 24/7 heart rate, HRV, skin temperature, and sleep, run seamlessly in the background, with automatic workout detection and a reliable seven-day battery life.

What truly elevates the experience is the integration of Google’s Health Coach AI, available through a Google Health Premium subscription, which is included for those who already have Google One. Food logging is a surprise standout—you can snap a picture of your meal, and the app accurately breaks down the macros. While the core tracking functions are available without a subscription, the real value comes from the Premium features like AI coaching, deeper analytics, and adaptive plans. But you don’t need the membership for the basic features.

Some trade-offs to consider: The Air is screenless, so you can’t check notifications or your heart rate on the go, which may take some getting used to for smartwatch users. The default band can feel a bit stiff, and you charge it off-wrist. Still, the Fitbit Air is a compelling option for those who want minimalist, screenless health tracking without a hefty commitment.

At $99 with no required subscription, it’s an accessible entry point for anyone who wants comprehensive health data and a genuinely helpful AI coach, especially those seeking a distraction-free, streamlined alternative to full-featured smartwatches.

Read More: Our Full Review of the Google Fitbit Air

Fitness trackers started as mostly wrist devices—from step counters like the Fitbit to the original Apple Watch. Now, you can choose from an array of fitness tracking watches or smart rings, all of which track far more than just your fitness. While most of these devices offer similar capabilities, the design you choose—watch or ring—will depend on your own personal aesthetic.

Do you prefer the look of a fitness tracker watch? Do you find it more convenient to navigate all your stats on your wrist? Or do you like the look of a ring better and prefer the sleeker, more compact design? If so, are you also agreeable to the fact that you will need to visit your phone app to view all of your stats? Only you have the answers to these personal questions.

Fitness trackers don’t just track fitness anymore. Sure, your workouts are still a key part of what these devices do, but in addition they’re also tracking your sleep, heart rhythm patterns, blood oxygen levels, HRV, stress, and even caffeine consumption. Fitness trackers are more of an overall wellness tool than ever before.

That said, the type of fitness tracker you choose should weigh heavily on the way you intend to use it. If you’re just looking for insights into your sleep cycle, you’ll probably want a smart ring that’s fairly comfortable to wear to bed each night. If you’re training for a marathon, you may want a watch with a touchscreen that’s easy to navigate mid-run. This last part may be especially important: If you’re already using certain tracking apps that you love (let’s say, Apple Fitness for instance), you’ll want to make sure you find yourself a fitness tracker that is compatible with that app in particular.

The accuracy of a fitness tracker begins and ends with how precisely it tracks your heart rate (most other stats, with the exception of new hydration tracking features, are derived from this baseline data). This mark is as much about pure accuracy as it is about consistency of accuracy, which is key in helping you understand your own fitness trends. While some trackers tend to be more accurate than others, all of the trackers we included here were consistently accurate.

With the exception of the Apple Watch Ultra 4 and the Samsung Galaxy Watch9 (two fitness trackers that are equal parts smartwatch but have seen improvement in their battery life), the fitness trackers we tested are all designed to run for days (even weeks) on a single charge. We love a fitness tracker that needs minimal charging, and we also love a fitness tracker that can run for a long time when running its GPS mode. Two fitness trackers that surprised us when testing battery life include the Suunto Race S (up to 13 days on a single charge) and the Garmin Instinct 3 (21 days on a single charge when set to its most basic mode).

All fitness trackers are made to take a beating, but some are better designed than others. Look at case construction, screen construction, and for smart rings, material. The most advanced fitness trackers (smart rings included) are typically made with stainless steel, titanium, or another type of quality metal. For screens, sapphire glass is a premium spec to look for thanks to its high scratch resistance and strength.

Sometimes the most durable fitness tracker is unnecessary, though, especially if you are using it for pavement running. In fact, a plastic construction fitness tracker can be a much better choice for cardio-intensive activities like running and biking, since the material is going to be much lighter than stainless steel or titanium. On the flip side, if you are using your fitness tracker for strength training (or hiking or trail running), it would make sense to look for stronger materials.

#4887. China's Z.ai Says Its New AI Model Can Think Like One Four Times Its Size

China's Z.ai Says Its New AI Model Can Think Like One Four Times Its Size

Chinese AI developer Z.ai released GLM-5.4 on Monday, introducing a new architecture that it claims delivers the reasoning capabilities of models nearly four times its size without requiring nearly as many parameters.

The Beijing-based company says its new 750-billion-parameter model can compete with systems containing two to three trillion parameters. It also supports a one-million-token context window, accepts images and video, and can generate up to 128,000 tokens in a single response, according to its technical documentation. Parameters are the internal values a model learns during training, while tokens are the chunks of text it processes and generates—roughly equivalent to words or parts of words.

The release comes as American companies including OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google dominate the highest positions in AI performance rankings. Chinese developers, meanwhile, have increasingly focused on making powerful models cheaper to run, particularly for coding and autonomous agents.

You can now act on the news you read: Go long or short Anthropic with perpetual futures, all from your Decrypt Money Account.

Z.ai has been among China's strongest competitors. As Decrypt reported in August, its predecessor GLM-5.3 was already competitive with leading American coding models, although it still trailed the best proprietary systems on several software engineering tests.

GLM-5.4 introduces what Z.ai calls a Loop Transformer, which processes information through the same 28 neural network layers twice. Traditional Transformers generally pass information through each layer once, so reusing layers effectively increases computational depth without increasing the number of stored parameters.

The company combines this approach with gated updates, which regulate how much information changes between passes, and a hybrid memory cache that avoids storing an entirely separate copy of previous calculations. Sparse attention also helps the model focus its computing power on relevant information rather than processing everything equally.

In controlled experiments on a smaller 30-billion-parameter model, Z.ai reported that its looping technique increased accuracy on AIME 2025, a mathematics examination benchmark scored by the percentage of correctly solved problems, from 19.17% to 30.42%.

GPQA Diamond, a graduate-level science question-answering test also scored by correct-answer percentage, improved from 47.58% to 56.26%. The experiments required approximately 10% additional training compute, while reducing generated reasoning tokens by 10% to 14% on several tests.

Those results concern the experimental architecture, not independently established performance for GLM-5.4 itself. Z.ai has not published a comprehensive flagship benchmark comparison demonstrating that the new model matches Claude or GPT.

The company is also promoting what it calls Generalized Coding: using programming to accomplish tasks beyond software development. Its demonstrations include building a video game, creating 3D environments, and designing a six-axis robotic arm using engineering software.

The company's previous flagship costs $1.40 per million input tokens and $4.40 per million output tokens through its API. By comparison, OpenAI's GPT-6.2 Sol costs $2 and $10, while Anthropic's Claude Opus 5.5 costs $4 and $20, respectively.

At those published rates, GLM-5.3 is considerably cheaper than its American counterparts, although GLM-5.4's own API pricing has not been announced. Its actual performance-per-dollar advantage therefore cannot yet be calculated.

China has other serious contenders. Alibaba's Qwen 4 Max recently topped Chinese competitors on Arena's agent leaderboard, while Moonshot's Kimi K3 and DeepSeek V4 have challenged American developers on coding performance and API pricing.

The performance gap remains contested. A U.S. government evaluation placed DeepSeek roughly eight months behind the American frontier, although researchers questioned its methodology.

Z.ai itself remains on the U.S. Commerce Department's Entity List, following its January 2025 designation over concerns that its AI research could support Chinese military modernization. The restrictions limit access to controlled American technology, but have not stopped the company from releasing new models.

#4886. Swiss central bank, UBS and SIX study ledger for tokenized settlement

The study will assess whether a shared ledger can bring central bank money, commercial bank money and securities onto the same platform.

The Swiss National Bank (SNB), UBS, SIX and other financial institutions announced a study Tuesday into whether a shared ledger could settle domestic and cross-border transactions involving tokenized assets.

Other participants include Zürcher Kantonalbank, PostFinance, Banque Cantonale Vaudoise, Basler Kantonalbank, Sygnum Bank and settlement platform Seturion, the central bank said in its announcement.

The proposed Swiss Financial Ledger would bring central bank money, commercial bank money and securities onto one platform. Participants will assess the benefits and risks, compare designs and examine how to govern and operate the ledger. The SNB said the study leaves open whether the ledger will launch or the central bank will issue tokenized money to financial institutions.

The initiative is part of the SNB’s Project Helvetia, which explores how to settle tokenized asset transactions in central bank money.

It also builds on Project Agorá, an initiative of the Bank for International Settlements and the Institute of International Finance, which explores whether tokenization can make wholesale cross-border payments faster, cheaper and safer.

#4884. The Best Lymphatic Drainage Tools in 2026

These may help to de-puff your face and body.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

You've seen enough GRWM videos to know that plenty of people are incorporating lymphatic drainage tools into their morning routines. If you also grapple with regular facial puffiness, it's fair to wonder if lymphatic drainage tools could help.

These tools are marketed to encourage the flow of lymph, which is a clear, watery fluid that supports fluid balance and immune defenses in your body. The idea with lymphatic drainage tools—which include gua sha stones, facial rollers, dry brushes, and more—is to mechanically encourage the movement of lymphatic fluid to reduce puffiness and fluid retention.

Dermatologists say there's something to this. "Your lymph system helps move excess waste and fluid throughout the body," explains Joshua Zeichner, MD, director of cosmetic and clinical research at Mount Sinai Hospital. "Lymphatic massages help enhance activity of the system to circulate excess fluid and depuff."

Doctors may recommend lymphatic drainage massage to people with health conditions like lymphedema or to post-surgical patients, says Ife J. Rodney, MD, founding director of Eternal Dermatology + Aesthetics. For everyone else, the data is less clear on whether lymphatic drainage offers much benefit.

"For everyday facial puffiness, gentle massage may offer temporary improvement, though research on gua sha is limited," Dr. Rodney says. Note the "temporary" part of what she said. Despite what you may hear on social media, lymphatic drainage won't permanently alter your cheekbones and jawline, although Dr. Rodney says this may help your skin look fresher and your features look more defined. "Think of it as an optional self-care step, not a requirement for healthy skin," Dr. Rodney says.

Want to opt into this? There is a range of tools to consider—and doctors say you don't need to spend a ton to get results.

A gua sha tool needs to have at least one smooth edge for lymphatic drainage. But Face Gym's Multi-Sculpt High-Performance Contouring Tool has six different edges to focus on different areas of your face. Long and medium smooth edges are made for classic lymphatic drainage, while shorter sides are ideal for areas around your eyes. The tool even includes rounded teeth for fine lines and a targeted tip for acupressure. (Face Gym has an instructional video to help guide you through all of this.)

Face Gym's gua sha tool is made from stainless steel, which is hypoallergenic and easy to clean. The stainless-steel tool is easy to clean, but any product can irritate skin. It also has a nice cooling effect after briefly popping it into the fridge. While this gua sha tool is on the pricier end, its stainless steel construction means it won't shatter like its crystal counterparts if you accidentally drop it.

Both are used for facial massage, but a roller moves across the skin by rolling, while gua sha uses sweeping strokes with a smooth-edged tool. You simply glide the roller across your face with gentle pressure to support lymphatic drainage. This is another stainless steel tool, which means it's a little more hygienic and easy to clean than its more traditional crystal versions. It's also highly likely to survive in the event of a drop.

The stainless steel has a natural cooling feel against the skin, although you can toss it in the fridge for extra chilliness. This tool is heavier than stone or crystal rollers—keep that on the back burner if you're used to those. It also pairs best with an oil or serum to help it glide smoothly over your skin, so plan to moisturize at the same time.

Dry brushing has the combined benefit of supporting lymphatic drainage and exfoliation at once. But some brushes have stiff bristles that can feel intense—and even irritating—against the skin. Esker's dry brush is gentle against the skin, but still feels firm enough to get things moving.

Esker's dry brush has a spa-like feel, along with an ergonomic design that's easy to grip. It's relatively petite, too, making it easy to toss in a bag for travel or trips to the gym. The only downside of that is the handle isn't long enough to reach your back. (That's more of an exfoliation issue vs. a lymphatic drainage problem, though.) The rules of lymphatic drainage still apply with a brush like this—if it feels irritating to your skin, stop. But if dry brushing is something you're into and you want to try lymphatic drainage on areas of your body other than your face, this is a solid choice.

Most people do gua sha on their face with a sculpting stone, crystal, or steel device. But not everyone is into the sensation of moving something hard across the sensitive skin in that area. Skin Gym answers the call with a face brush designed specifically for gua sha.

The brush is densely packed with soft bristles, creating a feel like you're swiping a really good makeup brush over your skin. It has an ergonomic shape, making it easy to glide over the contours of your face. This brush is pretty beginner-friendly, making it a good entry point if you're gua sha-curious. But be aware that you may not be able to get as much pressure on your skin (if that's even your thing) as you would with a stone or other hard tool. The brush also requires regular cleaning to avoid pushing dirt around your face.

Let's get this out of the way upfront: Vibration plates are a controversial tool. Many make bold claims, like that they can support weight loss or mimic the effects of exercise—those are unfounded. But these plates can improve circulation and lymphatic drainage as a result, as Rachelle Reed, PhD, an exercise scientist based in Athens, Georgia, previously told Women's Health.

FWIW, research suggests that you'll get many of the same circulation- (and lymph-)promoting effects from exercise. But if a vibrating plate is more your speed, the LifePro Waver is worth a look. This machine offers 99 speed levels to allow you to fine-tune the intensity. The motor is also quiet, so you don't have to stress about waking up other people in your home. This plate comes with resistance bands and free online workout videos, too. Just be aware that vibration plates aren't safe for everyone—you should take a pass if you're pregnant, prone to blood clots, or struggle with dizziness, for example. Bachelorette star Gabby Windey also recently shared that she broke her back after cranking up her (unnamed) vibrating plate in the name of lymphatic drainage. Though her account is an individual anecdote and doesn’t establish the typical risk, proceed with caution. But if you're looking for a way to get lymphatic drainage moving around your whole body, this is worth considering.

The best type of lymphatic drainage tool for you depends on where you plan to use it on your body. "These types of massages can be performed anywhere, but are extremely common on the face," Dr. Zeichner says. "Oftentimes, a smooth, but firm tool like gua sha is used."

A roller is another option for facial lymphatic drainage, and is usually considered more gentle than gua sha. For lymphatic drainage other than the face, some people reach for dry brushes and vibration plates.

For gua sha, Dr. Rodney suggests choosing a tool with smooth, rounded edges with no sharp spots, chips, or cracks. "It should be comfortable to hold and easy to clean," she says—and that applies to all lymphatic drainage tools. For gua sha and rollers, "you don't need an expensive crystal," Dr. Rodney says.

Dry brushes should also have natural fibers, since synthetic can feel harsh against the skin. Overall, "a smooth finish and gentle technique matter more than the price tag," Dr. Rodney says.

There's no exact weight for a lymphatic drainage tool that's best for everyone. Instead, it should feel substantial in your hand—but not too heavy or too light. You want to be able to comfortably use your tool, and allow the tool to apply pressure vs. heavy force from you.

They may temporarily reduce puffiness for some people, but evidence for at-home tools and broader health benefits is limited. "For everyday facial puffiness, gentle massage may offer temporary improvement," Dr. Rodney says. Dr. Zeichner agrees. "Especially if you wake up puffy in the morning after drinking too much alcohol, not having enough sleep, taking in too much salt, or during your menstrual cycle, lymphatic massages can be a useful tool," he says. Just keep in mind that the effects are temporary.

Every type of tool is slightly different. "The goal of a lymphatic massage is to improve the body's drainage system," Dr. Zeichner says. "That is why the massage should be performed in a particular way." If you're massaging your face, the pressure should start near the center of your face, before moving out and down toward your collarbones. This follows the natural lymphatic flow, Dr. Zeichner says.

"A few minutes is enough to start," Dr. Rodney says. "Keep the pressure light—bruising is a sign to stop, not a sign it’s working."

Dermatologists recommend taking a pass on lymphatic drainage tools if your skin is irritated, infected, sunburned, or broken. (That includes inflamed acne, eczema, or rosacea flares, per Dr. Rodney.)

Dr. Rodney also suggests checking with your doctor if you take blood thinners, bruise easily, or have blood clots, heart or kidney failure, lymphedema, or a history of cancer treatment or lymph node removal. "After cosmetic procedures or surgery, get clearance from your treating clinician," she says. "Persistent or painful swelling needs an explanation before it needs a massage."
